===== 0002601_19020829_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FREE CHITRCHES:

A MOMENTODS DECISION.

The vital part of Clause VII. of the Educe. lion Bill, providing that each Voluntary school diall have six managers (four clerically appointed and two representative), has been forced through the House of Commons by the Prime Minister Free Churchmen feel that this arrangement is a direct challenge to them. It has been sug- gested in some quarters that the National Free Church Council shoukl immediately organise a no-rate campaign. On the other hand, many Free Churchmen—and these not the least reso- lute in their antagonism to the Bill—doubt the wisdom of such a campaign, either on the ground of policy or on the ground of timeliness. In these circumstances the General Committee of the National Council felt that it could not, without Consultation with the Councils, com- mit them to a policy in this matter. Accord- ingly a referendum of the eight hundred Coun- cils is now being taken. The committee has entered into the matter of the no-rate cam- paign most exhaustively, and carefully tabulates the arguments that are urged on both sides. The fact that this statement of the case is warmly approved by leading representatives of both sections of opinion in the matter testifies conclusively to the perfect impartiality with which this document has been drawn up. The result of this appeal to the local Councils will probably be made known in October.

===== 0002601_19020829_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
DR. PARKER.

Dr. Parker has been recuperating at the country house of Mrs Burnett Smith, the well- known authoress, in Buckinghamshire. Mrs. Smith (better known as Annie S. Swan) is at present in Canada, where her novels have won her troops of friends, especially among the Scotch settlers. Dr. Parker and Mrs. Smith live near to each other in Hampstead. The novelist was one of the most intimate friends of the late Mrs. Parker, and wrote a beautiful tribute to her memory in the British Weekly.

===== 0002601_19020829_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
DL GUNS/MIMS, OF CHICAGO.

Dr. Parker hopes to resume his ministry at the City Temple towards the close of Septem- ber. The talk about his successor that has been prevalent lately is merely wild nonsense. The famous pulpit is not vacant, and though Dr. Parker is now seventy-two, will not be vacant, we hope, for years. Dr. Gunsaulus has been a very acceptable "supply,? but it is at least doubtful whether his ornate and voluminous style would suit the English taste. One acute observer has discovered a resemblance in per- sonal appearance between Dr. Gunsaulus and Gipsy Smith, the famous evangelist.

===== 0002601_19020829_art0021.txt | included=true | included =====
MARK GUY PRANSE.

The return of the Rev. Mark Guy Pearse to work at the St. James's Hall, Piccadilly, will be enthusiastically welcomed in many quarters. Mr. Pearse for years conducted the morning service there, and won great popularity, particu- larly among young people. The West End of London is, at the best, a dreary place on Sunday morning, and when the weather is bad, the gloom and desolation are almost unspeakable. But _Mr. Pearse was always in high spirits, always cheering and comforting. It is an- rouiced that he will again occasionally take laie /earning service. It would be a delight to many if he would take it always. Mr. Price Hughes, who is at present in Switzerland, hope to resume his ministry in September.

===== 0002601_19020829_art0022.txt | included=true | included =====
FREE CHURCH PARLIADIRNTARY RECRUITS.

Mr. George Toulmin, M.P., and Mr. Rowland Barran, M.P., the victors of Bury and North Leeds respectively, are both Nonconformists' the former a Wesleyan, and the latter a Bap- tist. Mr. Toulmin is keenly interested in Sun- day-school work and nothing gave him greater delight at the time of his election than a letter of congratulation from the school of which he is superintendent. The two new M.P.s are within a year of each other in age, the former being forty-five, and the latter forty-four. But they look ten years younger.

===== 0002601_19020829_art0023.txt | included=true | included =====
WHO IS PETER PICKLE ?

The latest literary puzzle concerns itself with the identity of Peter Pickle, the pseudonym of the writer of some strikingly frank articles now appearing in the Free Church Chronicle. Mr. Pickle has been writing on "What makes a good meeting" and "On preparing a sermon." In the latter article he warns preachers against making a parade of their learning. "If the congregation," he says, "is composed of labourers and working people generally, the preacher should never on any account refer to Plato or Aristotle or Epictetus. The good

folks are not at all edified by such allusions, not even for long impressed thereby. Souls aFannot be nourished by mystification any more than on the east wind. A preacher once remarked in the course of a ponderous and weighty discourse : Amicus Plato, amicus Socrates, sed major veritas.' A good proportion of his congregation, as they walked home, were deeply puzzled by the strange conduct of Major Veritas. Why should he want to cuss' Plato or Socrates? What harm had they done to him? And who were these same persons, Plato or Socrates? They had never been heard tell of in that parish."

===== 0002601_19020829_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
REV. THOMAS LAW.

Rev. Thomas Law, the General Secretary of the Free Church Council, who is organising the campaign against the Education Bill through- out the country, had purposed to spend August at Llandudno with his family, but the exigencies of business brought Mr. Law, Dr. Clifford, and other Free Church leaders to London for the greater part of last week to confer together -concerning the next step in the agitation. The result of their deliberations will be seen in the autumn, when the campaign will be resumed with increased vigour and determination.

===== 0002601_19020829_art0025.txt | included=true | included =====
FREE CHURCHMEN AT THE CORONATION.

Various eminent Nonconformists who were present at the "great solemnity" have pub. lished their impressions of the ceremony. Perhaps the most interesting are Dr. ToTrn., send's. Dr. Townsend was struck with the tall, stalwart, graceful figures of the English aris- tocracy, male and female. He considers, how- ever, that the beauty of the women was dis- appointing. There was a deterioration from the time when the Duchess of Sutherland (Queen Victoria's friend) was the belle of Britain.

===== 0002601_19020829_art0026.txt | included=true | included =====
ILLUSTRATED FASHIONS.

There are a great number of little dainty articles of dress which are really necessary for the success of a toilette, and to these some women devote very little attention, with the result that many an otherwise smart turn-out is altogether spoiled. There are always many odd bits of lace and insertion, silk and satin left over from blouses and dainty gowns for which at the first glance there seems no ap- parent use. A careless woman would consign such to the rag bag, but one with clever fingers would evolve from these oddments just the dainty trifles, such as fronts, yokes, or neck arrangements, which will be found of incalcu- lable use at times. Of course no hard and fast rules can be laid down for the making of these pretty trifles which play so large a part in the dress of to-day. One thing, however, is essential (and this is a fact often overlooked by the amateur worker), namely, a scrupulous neatness in finishing off the details. Fronts should have tape braces behind to bold the neck piece down tightly, for otherwise it is sure to get out of place. All fronts should be cut with ample width across the front coming well under the armhole. Many of the prettiest blouses this season have sailor collars, made either of the material itself edged with a lace or silk frill, or of lace entirely, or silk trimmed with lace. To make a successful sailor collar it should be cut all in one piece about twenty to twenty-four Inches ix width to give plenty of spring. The

PRETTY BLorsz. pretty blouse sketched has a collar of this description. It is made of pale blue washing silk, and the lace with which it is trimmed is of an ecru tint. This colour of lace is very fashionable, but has the somewhat annoying peculiarity that it washes white, a fact every- one is not aware of till experience brings enlightenment. A blouse or dress trimmed with this tone of lace is therefore much better sent to the cleaners than the laundry when soiled. A dainty fiehu of silk muslin or lace is often a welcome addition to a summer toilette. A very simple plan for making a Sohn is to drape a piece of late-edged muslin round the shoulders, hollowing out a few darts at the neck to make it sit well.

One of the prettiest summer dresses I have seen this season was of white foulard pin-spotted with black, and further graced by a lovely design of pink blush roses. The bodice boasted of a deep and handsome lace collar, with an inner waistcoat of pale pink accordion pleated chiffon embroidered with silver. White is wonderfully popular, considering how trying it is to most complexions, and there are many women still who sensibly enongh refuse to bow to the whims of Madame la Mode, and prefer to stick to the deep cream canvas frieze, cloth or wincey gowns which look so smart and become them so well.

The charming hat depicted is of a light cream straw trimmed with lilac figured foulard, the brim edged with lace. The shape has a wide brim which affords very comfortable shade to the eyes on a glaring hot day. Hats for the seaside should have as little colour as possible about them, for sun and sea-air combined have a bleaching effect and ruin utterly the delicate shades of mauve, pink, blue and green we adore. Black combined and lightened by white are beloved by the Parixiennes, and many are the beautiful draped-0a which reach us from

18 Wigan away 25 Widnes ,--..--. THE TRIALS OF A DEERSTALKER.

BM there is always the chance, perhaps to be named the probability, that the stag:will move. You will lose sight of him as you go up the glen. Therefore the gillie with the pony is bid to remain where he is, with instruotions,and a spy-lass. The spy-glass is to enable him to see if the stag goes over the march, and the inetructioos are that he shall tie his handkerchief (and a 011ie has a handkerchiet) to the stump of an old tree, there to rsmain as long as the stag shall remain, thence to be removed in the event of the stag's removing him- self acres. the boundary. Then yea go up the glen with hope beating high. The man, the pony, and the land- !turbid fall cut of sight as you go on, but ater a mile of steals, walking along the relatively fiat, or only gently rising ground, the stalker, with a motion •1 bus band, silentlf bidding you follow him, turns upward to the left, and you begin to mount. The first point that gives you a view of the keoll from which the Stag was spied is gained in some perturbation of mind ; but all, so far, is well, the handkerchief re- mains, and to, too, the stag. Again you mount the steep incline. Again and again an anxious glance ecuthward shows the handkerchief still in place, aid hope constantly beats higher, until, without warning, the stalker suddenly gives eWence that be has more English at command than you had expected by exclaim- ing. " Damn " Then, as you glance towards him with inquiry, "The handkerchief is down." It is true. It is a ead fae. N. prolonged gazing through the glass can reproduce that handkerchief. It has gene, and so, teo,ic must be assumed, has the stag. There is no more to say. The stalker has said all there is to say for you, politely. It remains only to " take a sit," as he expresses it, and have a spy, to see if any more beasts suitable can be %led from the higher ground now reached.—From " On a Forest," in the " Cornhill Magazine " for Sep- tember.

THE EXAMINER FRIDAY AUGUST 29 1902

[ALL RIGHTS RESERVED.]

A QUESTION OF TEMPERAMENT

BY MARY L. CAMERON.

PART 111.-CHAPTER

Next morning Antony appeared smiling and imperturbable as ever. He answered inquiries after Mrs. Haig, saying that she intended lunching in the mkt, and that he hoped then to have the pleasure of making her known to them all. A thrill of curiosity of different degrees of intensity went through the members of the little circle when a tall woman in a Parisian costume came into the room. She seemed to be about forty years of age, and her dress fitted without a flaw—a fine figure, rather too drawn in at the waist and expanding over the bust and hips, for statuesque beauty, but perfect from the dressmaker's point of view. Her nose was long and her eyes small, nevertheless she was a handsome woman, her bright colour, even teeth, full red lips, and curved, well-marked eye- brows making up a striking ensemble. Her hair was waved all over her head, and low down over her forehead, and its colour was perhaps a little out of harmony with the tones of her complexion and eyes. She came forward with outstretched hands. "So delighted to make the acquaintance of Tony's friends. He has written me such accounts of you, I feel I know you already. It is so good of you to have kept him amused, for I am weeks overdue. It is too bad of me. I agreed to meet him here on the 20th of July, and now it is the middle of August, and the dear fellow has been waiting all that time. The fact is, the bathing at Cabourg was doing me so much good, and my dearest friend the Countess La Cour de Is Vigne arrived just when I ought to have been leaving, and a crowd of other delightful people were there, and they positively would not hear of me going. So when Tony wrote that two of the most interesting girls he ever met (and he is always meeting inter- esting girls) were staying at his hotel, I felt I could remain away without feeling too heavy a load on my conscience." Mrs. Haig chattered away without waiting for a reply. "And you, Mr. Coverdale, Tony's old college chum. I know all about you, too I Admirable! An example to all the idle young men one sees about, only of course they won't follow it. Ah ! no, this is a selfish world! Well, at any rate we can all enjoy ourselves here without a scruple. Holiday time and the beauties of Nature all around us, innocent pleasures are the only ones to be had." They sat down to dejeuner, and Mrs. Haig kept the ball of Conversation rolling all the time. After- wards she asked what were the plans for the afternoon.

"Can we drive to some ruin or cave or shrine? There are always such places in mountainous neighbourhoods that one can make a pretence of being interested in. They do very well as the object for a drive, though of course one doesn't want to see them in the least."

" We generally walk," suggested Catherine. "Oh! I know you are admirably energetic. But I never walk, it does not agree with me, and it spoils one's shoes," and she looked at the tip of a high-heeled kid shoe embroidered with steel beads, which peeped out from under her skirt. "You must all drive with me. Just to give me a treat. Now do."

When Mrs. Haig set her mind on anything she generally carried her point, and after some dis- cussion, a ruined castle in a not too inaccessible situation was thought of, and a carriage ordered to convey the party to it. On arriving at their destination it was found, much to Mrs. Haig's relief, that the ruins consisted only of the crumbling remains of an old keep, so there was nothing for the most conscientious sightseer to explore. They, therefore, settled themselves on a shady slope, and proceeded to enjoy the contents of a basket of fruit and cake which the organizer of the expedition had taken care should be put in the carriage. . . . .

The hoes were put up at a little inn in a village at the foot of the hilt on which the ruins stood, and orders given to the coachman to be ready for them in a couple of hours. When Mrs. Haig was comfortably settled with shawls and cushions to protect her from possible damp or insects, she said to Catherine : "Come and sit beside me, Miss Wood. I want to hear all about your career. Tony tells me you are so clever. Quite one of the modern advanced girls, that one doesn't meet in society, because they despise it. Do tell n/e what you have to make up for all that ordinary girls call fun ?" Catherine obeyed, inwardly amused, and Harry Coverdale came and leaned against a tree beside them, and helped Catherine out in answering the flow of questions with which she was assailed. Meanwhile Isobel had wandered away gathering flowers. Antony followed her, but she seemed absorbed in her occupation, and took no notice of him. After walking about beside her for a time, stopping when she stopped, and moving on as she moved, he said: "What do you tbink of Theo ? " "She is very bright and good-natured." "Of course she is. I don't want to be told that. I mean what opinion have you formed of her? Do you think she is wholly and wilfully frivolous, or do you think it is a pose, to hide her want of education? Do you think she is clever enough to see that she gains a sort of power through seeming more foolish than she is?"

Isobel was determined to express neither surprise nor disappointment at any of Antony Haig's vagaries for the future, so she said, coldly: I daresay she is cleverer than she appears at first sight." "Ah 1 I see you don't trust me. Perhaps you are wise. Men always do tell women what other women say of them, and with unpleasant, results. Still, I should have liked to know your real impression of Theo. I have a very high opinion of your critical insight, Miss Knight." Sore and angry as Isobel was feeling, she almost laughed at this. He went on: "I feel more and more the need I have of some one of sound judgment and—how shall I express it —subtle, unassertive influence, near me. I- am so sensitive to the moods of those about me. I have grown to depend very much on your sympathetic responsiveuess,—l hardly realised how much till last night." Isobel said nothing. "When I saw Theo's boxes, do you know, I felt quite a pang, really a painful sensation. Not that I was sorry Theo had come. lam always glad to see her. It was the thought that our daily routine of constant companionship was broken in on, that partings were in the air, and that what was past, was past." "Where do you and Mrs. Haig go when you leave here?" The canality of the question angered Isobel, but for the life of her she could think of nothing 'better to say to break the awkward silence.

"I don't know. Theo will want to go to some health resort, somewhere to drink waters or bathe, or do something for her figure or complexion, and meet fashionable people, and I Shall refuse and we shall quarrel a little; we always do quarrel when we have been together a week or so. And then we shall part • she will go off to her resort, and I shall wander to some old, medimval city or quaint hamlet on an enervating plain, and dwell in an old house or deserted convent near a malarial swamp; I hate healthy, bracing places! I only came here to meet Theo, and it has tfeen your companionship that has given it all its charm for me; for it is just the sort of place I detest and always avoid." Isobel was busy binding her flowers together with a blade of long crass; she looked at Antony for the first time since he had joined her. He was not watching her as he usually did when he talked, so she could observe himiknore narrowly. He looked pale and anxious, and his long white fingers were nervously interlaced. FeT a moment a feeling pity took her by surprise, then she despised her own softness and put an icier chill into her voice as she answered:

"Everything has its compensating advantage. You will now be able to choose the sort of a place you like to be in, and so not need my improving society. Besides, who can say? You may meet quite as congenial confipanions there, and so be suited in every way." "That is more than unlikely," began Antony. But Isobel had had enough of this unprofitable talk, and began to walk quickly back towards the rest of the party, and Antony was fain to follow her. They found that Catherine and Harry had made up their minds to walk home, and Isobel would have given much to be able to join them, butNipart from the incivility of abandoning the Haigs entirely, she knew too well that she should feel an uncomfortable third if she went with the others, and would spoil the pleasure of their walk. It was possibly nearly their last walk together, for Coverdale's holiday was nearly at an end, and he talked of leaving the next (lay or the day after. Under these circumstances Isobel felt there waa

no release for her, and she must harden herself to play her part to the end. Mrs. Haig appeared to have had something to do with sending the others away; she smilemeaningly as they walked off with an air of serene contentment in each other's company. As Isohel saw them disappear between the pine. trees, a miserable feeling of abandonment came over her ; she felt as if she were one solitary little person in the midst of a desert. She glanced towards Mrs. Haig, who was talking confidentially to Antony with her hand on his shoulder. The sense of isolation became almost unbearable. Everybody was happy and full of their own affairs, only she was left out in the cold. She sat down on a tree-trunk with her back to them, and looked down the valley. The beauty seemed to have gone out of the landscape; the pine woods looked monotonous, and the distant peaks hard and cruel. Then she heard Mrs. Haig calling her. She turned round; Antony was walking away towards the little auberge in the valley, where the carriage was put up. "I have sent Tony to order the carriage, Miss Knight, and we will walk gently down by that little green path to meet it, if you are willing." Isobel rose without enthusiasm, and they walked slowly on together. "I am so glad to have this chance of a quiet little chat with you, Miss Knight. For, „do you know, Ido not think I shall stay here long. It is beautiful, of course, and, no doubt, very healthy for those it suits, but I feel it is not going to suit me. should be ill if I stayed here, I know I should, uid to fall ill here without any of the comforts one is u3ed to ! And the cooking being—well, what it is! So, on the whole, I think of going on to Bordone en ronte for Ischl to-morrow.

"Thu sooner the better," thought Isobel. "I could not stand much more of his kind of thing." But she said— "l am sorry our acquaintance should be so short, but no doubt your decision is a wise one." "It must not end here though, that is what I wanted to talk to you about. It has been such a pleasure to me to see how well you and Tony get on together that I should be in despair if I thought I had interrupted or interfered with the develop- ment of your friendship." Isobel thought this view of the situation even stranger than that of Antony; she was saved the trouble of inventing a reply as Mrs.- Haig went on—

" I am going to be quite frank with you, I know you will understand and appreciate my motives. That dear fellow is sometimeg an anxiety to me. He has strange ideas. In anyone else I should call them cranks and fads, but in him they are only the natural outcome of his delightfully original nature. Still they make him unlike other people and cause him to hate all the things and people, that make up the only society I think worth living in. For I must confess that I cannot be happy amongst people who wear grange clothes and eat meals of vegetables and cocoa. I can appreciate Ibsen and Maeterlinch and even Verlaine (some- times), for lam quite moderne' and unprejudiced, but I like a good ballet or burlesque too, and a recherché little dinner first and a toilette that comes from Paris. So you will easily understand that these fancies of Tony's make me uneasy, and I am always in dread of what he may do next, espe- cially with regard to some of his lady friends. He has such very strange ones." And Mrs. Haig lr:aned over and tried to look down into Isobel's eyes, who kept them carefully averted. "Now, Miss Knight, you have no unorthodox views about marriage, have you ? " Isobel felt her head going round; she began to think she had strayed into Wonderland.' "Oh dear no!" she answered, hastily. • "I felt sure of it. You don't look as if you had. That is such a comfort. For I don't mind telling you that Tony has had friends—girls—women who have talked in the most unpractical;way about the immorality of marriage, and free unions, and all that nonsense, and I have had to exercise no little diplomacy to get Tony out of their influence. He is the most innocent, harmless dear fellow, if left to himself. Too much so, indeed—for a man. But one never knows what that sort of talk may lead to and—er—anything of that!sort, done in that way, defying the world, you know, is ruin to a young man. People don't mind him being out and out bad, they understand that, but when he is as harmless as a mouse in reality as Tony is—then they are after him like a pack of jackals. Now I am not going to let Tony be hounded out of society, not while I live, even though looking after his interests upsets my plans and causes me a good deal of worry and anxiety." Isobel walked on, feeling she was getting further and further into Wonderland. Mrs. Haig remained silent for a few moments, and then suddenly stand- ing still she put her arm through Isobel's and laid her other hand on her shoulder.

"But with you I should feel he was quite safe, my dear, and when (as I know he intends to do) he asks you to marry him, you will not refuse to make him happy, will you? You are exactly suited to each other. You do mean to accept him, don't you?" "Accept Mr. Haig. Accept your husband," gasped Isobel, fairly aghast. "My husband Good gracious! Poor Tony! He has made a mesa of things this time," and Mrs. Haig burst into a rippling laugh. "Tony is my step-son. How like him to go dreaming on, never seeing what you were thinking." "Your step-son! How stupid of me! Please forgive." "Don't apologise. The mistake is always being made. But I did think in this case that Tony wouldhave explained our relationship before I came, at any rate to you. But I suppose it never occurred to him, He lives with his head in a cloud, poor dear fellow, and he has been making himself quite wretched about your change of manner. He was just telling me that he believed you had taken a dislike to him; that was what decided me to speak to you, for I felt there must be some mistake. Do you know after all I am not sure that the rani- understanding has not its useful side, if, as I hope, it has been your sole reason fOr snubbing poor Tony. He was just going on in hi usual easy-going way enjoying your society with no thought for the morrow, and, until I came I don't believe any com- fortable sensible result had occurred to him. I saw at once that you were just the person I had been longing for to take the responsibility off my hands, but it was your coldness that woke him up and roused him into confiding his troubles to me. He has been so used to being spoil e 4 that a real rebuff was just what he needed to, male him value yoa at your true worth. But my dear, he will be here directly, and before he comes I want to explain a few things to you. He will be sure to make some more extraordinary bungles if I leave it to him. His father and he, as I daresay he has told you, did not get on at all. His ways and ideas were hateful to dear old John Haig who had made all his money in some nasty smelling dye or drug, and the old man just married me I do believe, to spite Tony. I was a penniless girl, with no educa- tion, so I had no choice, and I didn't know Tony till after my marriage as he was not on speaking terms with his father. In less than a year old John died (a merciful release my dear, gout and such a temper), leaving all his fortune to me. I sought out Tony at once and we became excellent friends • with his sweet disposition he was inca- pable Of bearing malice. At first I wanted to make a leaal gift, or whatever you call it, to him of half the fortune (my will is made in his favour); but when I saw what his friends were like and how unsuspicious he is, and as I have no opinion of people with unconventional views about marriage, I came to the conclusion that it would be best for us to live together and share and share alike, each taking whatever meney they wanted from the com- mon stock, until I saw him happily and suitably married. What I want you to understand is that on his marriage he will have half his father's fortunesand on my death the whole. I regard it as his right. Old John had no business to act as he did, and I should regard myself as a sneak if I did not undo his injustice." Isobel returned the affectionate squeeze The* Haig save to her arm, and said: ..

"You are a very kind anci generous friend. Let me beg your forgiveness. I have been judging you rather uncharitably."

"I saw it, my (fear. But I bore you no I know I don't understand all these high ways of living and thinking. I often tell Tony so. I was not well brotight up and I am fearfully ignorant. But I have my own ideas of what is right, and I do like to see people happy." So saying, she kissed Isobel on the cheek. Isobel felt that her world had been turned inside out since the morning; her head swam; she was incapable of judging anything coolly, nevertheless joy had come back and the dark shadow had gone from the landscape.

When the girls were alone together that night Catherine sat down on the floor at Isobel's feet and, resting her arm on her knees, looked up in her face.

"Do I look like a very bold and shameless hussy," she said.

"Ton rook very excited and very pretty," said Isobel, looking at her attentively. "What does all this mean

"it means that I have done a bold and shameless act. I proposed to Harry Coverdale to-day on the walk home and he accepted me." She laid her faee against her friend's knee and laughed a happy comfortable little laugh. "He needed that, did he ? " said Isobel. . .

"He did, indeed: He had a foolish idea that he had no right to ask anyone to share the life he has given himself up to, and he would despise himself as much as I should despise him, if he were to give it up. It was only when I made him understand that he had made a convert of me and that I was going to lead the life anyway, or rather such a poor imitation of it as I am capable of, that he would think of me at all, and even then he was too modest to believe it possible that I should be utterly wretched without his love. However, I made him understand it all somehow, and everything is all right." "You are notgoing to let him kill hlnself quite to fast as he is doing at present, I suppose. Well, 7011 are just the wife he wants,—you have life and mergy and faith-to beautify the sordid present. sunbeam goes with you everywhere, You will x loved, and-that will be all the reward you want. I see your future quite clearly, and it will be all right. I give you my blessing." Then after a pause, she leant down and, nearly pringing her lips to Catherine's ear, she said: "I am going to marry Antony Haig." Catherine sprang to her feet at one bound. "Marry Mr. Haig! Is he a Mormon? How many are there to be of you ? " " She isn't his wife. She is his step-mother !" Catherine clapped her hands over her head. "Oh I you sly little Isobel ! You knew it all the time."

"No, I didn't, not till to-day." " Oh ! what fun ! And what does mamma say?" "She is delighted. She wants me to take him )ff her hands, that she may go her own gay way." "And Mr. Haig? Is he really in love? He is very quiet and discreet about it." "He does not show his hand as poor Mr. Cover- dale has been doing for the last week, but " still waters run deep," you know. After all it is a luestion of temperament."

THE END.

THF SEPTEMBER "SMART SET."

—"A Friend of Cara's," by Louise Botts Edwards, the novelette with which the September " Smart Set " opens, is a character study at once very distinctive and very strong. The analysis is subtle in its revealment of a wontan's nature,while the rapidity of the action makes every page of absorbing interest to the reader. The story is .ne of the best yet published in " The Smart Set's" long list of successful novelettes. " The Story of Jess Uck," by Jack London, is a tale of Alaskan life, the history of a renunciation ; and in it the distinguished author has written one of the best, if net the best sustained effort of his career. The story is faseinatiag, and of profound power. Both in its comp- lies sad in its expression it is a master piece. Another story of especial worth but in lighter vein is " The Shining Gloss," by Gertrude Lynch, in which the psychology of beauty's attitude towards itself is most cunnin fly and clearly portrayed* James Branch Cabe!l contributes a delightful story, entitled " As the Coming of Dawn," and Emery Pottle writes " The Youthfulness of Wilkins," a clrver social study. A story of remark- able intrest, entitled " The Logic of Circumstances," is from the pen ef the late James Hazleton Willard, while Justus Miles Forman contributes" An Exnerimen'; of Jimmy Rogers's." and there is a dainty idyll," In a Bachelor's Garden," by Temple Bailey. An article written with a beauty worthy of its subject is, " Newport, the Apostle of Estheticism," by Douglat Story, in which the author seriously interprets the sig nificanea of Atnerican society's Summer heme. The postal of the cumber, which, whether grave or gay, are all of unusual excellence, are contributed by Bliss Carman, James Jeffrey Roche, Clinton Seollard, Victor Plarr, Frank Dempster Sherman, John Vance Cheney, Samuel Minturn Peck, ft EC Munkittrick, Albert Lee, Theadosia Garrison, Zona Gale and others.

SUSPECTED MURDER OF A WARRINGTON MAN.

Shocking Discovery in Natal,

News has been received of the suspected murder at Hee Cape of a single man named James O'Connor, aged 37 years, who formerly resided with his parents at 18, Friar'r-green, Warrington. The deceased was a joiner, and served his appreeticethip with the late Mr John Edward Wright. In 1890, however, be left for South Africa, and was employed at the mines near Johannes- burg. Upon tha Boer war breaking cur he had a natural dosire to fight for the country of his birth, and saw ser- vice with the Imperial Light Horse. Now that the war I. over and the country settling down .cornos the tragic news of his death, the details of which are contained in the following extract from "The Natal Mercury," dated Wednesday, July 23rd :--" The Maritzburg police were yesteirday busy carrying on inquiries concerning the death of a man whose body was found on the previous afternoon in the long grass, at the side of York-road, not far from the Cremorne Hotel. Constable Mus- grove discovered the body. The skull was smashed, as though the man bad been eruck on the top of the head with some heavy instrument, and there were evidetcss that he had been lEcked about the body. Spread out under the head was a Manehestot mar. It was ascertained that the deceased, a man of about 515 7in in height, and 34 years of age, was Jame O'Ocnaor, and had been in the Imperial Light Horse. He was wearing a grey quit, army ammunition boots, and s soldier's belt. rhe pockets WACO turned out, but en the body or near by were found a tobacco pouch, 3d. a box of matches, a screwdriver, and a foot rule. It is evident that the bady must have been carried some dis- tance to the spot where it was found. O'Connor had visited Cremorne Hotel frequently of late, and on Sun- day afternoon was taere with acquaintances. some at the latter have communicated with the police, and state that O'Connor left the hotel at nine o'clock on Sunday night. The landlord, however, denies this statement, de- claring that at that hour he, with his family, were tubed. The pollee have also been informed that O'Connor had an altercation with a man at the hotel during the evening. Inspector Barron, with Sergr ant Williamson sad Detective hmith of the C.1.D., made an inspection of the spot, and plans were prepared. Sergeant-Detec- tive Russell and others are also engaged in the case. The circumstances point to a deliberate murder, but it Is possible that the death may he the outcome of a brawl, and that the assailants turned out deceased's pockets in order to throw off suspicion from themselves. On s farther search of the body a sum of money was found.

The deceased's father lute had confirmation of these particulate from another source,and there is no diubt to their accuracy.

MILL OPERATIVES' DIFFICULTIES. Trouble in a Lancashire District.

Since the days when a former Earl of Shaftesbury laboured for the amelioration of the working classes, and when Richard Oastler, the Factory wing, as he was called, sought to benefit the workers in cotton mills. many important improvements have taken place in the life of the toiler. Time was when little children were employed in mills and workshops longer hours than grown-up men and women are now engaged, and when the life of women in the matter of work was one long drudgery. Happily in the present day this is changed. but there are great improvements still to make. Close sonfinement in the heated atmosphere of our cotton mills in Lancashire and eliewlaere is apt to rob the fram6 of its elasticity and the cheek.of the toiler of its bloom, the bloodless look observable in the faces of the cotton opera- tives telling all toe plainly the story of the need for the " walk which costs a meal," immortalised in the poem by flood," The Seng of the Shirt." The point may be emphasised by an illustration. At Astley Bridge, Bolton, there is a mill operative, Miss Hannah Duxbury, aged twenty-three, residing at 8. Mackenzie-street, who for some time was a sufferer from indigestion, bloodlessness, and the numerous distressing symptoms which accompany them, but fortunately, in this case, a complete cure was effected by Dr. Williams' pink pile. But let her tell her own tale, as elicited from her by a local reporter. " I must tell you," she said, " that I used to suffer very much thrmAgh weak— liest of the stomach, and being short of blood I was compelled to eansult two doctors. They were kind and treated me skilfully, but yet my improvement was not marked. My breath was short, I could not endure the least exertion, and I became weak'and miserable through lack of nourishment, which the indigestion prevented me taking."

" And what brought about a cure ?" queried the rirocrter.

" Well, I saw a newspaper article concerning Dr Williams' pink pills for pale people, and I asked my mother te get me some. She did so, and I began taking them. Before I had taken tho second box I felt ranch better. I had been off Env work as a carder in a cotton mill, following my doctor's orders, but before I had finished taking the second box, however, I felt so much improved that I was able to go back to my work. I continued taking them, and I took five boxes altogether They quite cured me. That is some two years ago. The shortness of breath disappeared, my colour became better, and I put on flesh. I have had slight touched of indigestion occasionally since then, but a dome or two of Dr. Williams' pink pills always cures me. Whenever I feel myself run down—and you know working in cotton mill does run one down—l at a box. In fact I am so enthusiastic about the pills that my friends some- times laugh at me for my earnestness. I feel all right now, thanks to them."

ILLUMINATED CAR IN COLLISION. Police Court Sequel at Warrington.

A Tail-Light Question.

At the Warrington Police Court on Wednesday, before Alderman Fairclough and other magistrates, Riohard Thomas, 10, Mason-etreet, was summoned that, on August 12th, at 11.10 p.m., being the driver of a cart Used for carryirg timber, he did not keep a lighted lamp at the rear of the cert." Mr Stanley Wilson (deputy- town clerk) cenduered the prosecution ant defendant Was represented by Mr W J Forshaw. Mr. Wilson said defendant was a driver in the employ of Mr R A Naylor, timber merchant, Howley. The charge was taken under the by-laws 1 'With respect to lighte on vehicles which required that a cert used between an hour after sunset and an hour Wore sunrise for the purpose of carrying timber should be provided with a lamp show- ing a red light at the rear, visible within a reasonable distance to persons overtaking the cart. About 11.10 cm August 12,h one of the illuminated cars was proceeding from Latehford to Warrineton. While mounting the Incline at Black Bear Bridge, Keutsford-road, the car driver noticed an obstruction in the road within a few yards in front of him, He at once shut off Dower and applied the brake, but in spite of this the c.r came into collision with what afterwards turned out to , be a huge tree which was being conveyed on 11 a timber waggon, drawn by three horses in charge of the defendant. _At the time, several witteesses would state, that they were unable to to see any tight from the rear,but on closer examination /tweet found that :there was a • lamp fixed on tree about 15 feet from the tail end. The tree measured 45 feet in length. The lamp had elan tipped ever sothat iz was impossible for anyone to see a light which was only an hob in circumference. Had it been possible, to see the light, it would have been deceptive as it was 15 feet from the tail. The damage done to the car and to the driver was slight, but if the driver had not been an expert the accident might have been very serious indeed. He need hardly point nut the importance of the bylaws leeing properly carried out, but it was only fair to Mr Naylor to say that so far as the firm was concerned they were anxionia and took all neleasary steps to have the bylaws carried OW. Evidently the driyar was at fault in this cal'. r Joseph Edward llndle, electrician in the employ of the Corporation, state I that lie was driving the oar in question,whieh was going at the rare of about six miles an hour. He noticed the end of the tree when it was about three yards away. Ho applied the brakei but the collision occurred and witness's band was crushed. He did not gee any light before the collision. Mr Forshaw asked witness if the brightness of the illuminations wou'd not dszzle him. Witness repiied that it would not, in fact he thought it would aid him. Mr. Forshaw submitted that the bylaw bad been com- plied with absolutely, because defendant placed a lamp on the timber which showed a red light in the rear and a White light in front. Ile would prove that the light was seen some considerable distance away. He submitted that the one lamp was quite sufficient, provided that it won/d light the rear as well ,as the front. Further, the bylaw stated that if a cart wag Used for the purpose of carrying timber or any Toed pro- jecting more than six feet to the rear, it should be pro- vided with a lamp capable of being so attached as to show to the rear a red light visible within a reasonable distance to persona overtaking the cart. The timber in question projected only 2i feet frem the waggon and therefore there was no necessity fora light at the rear. Mr Wilson submitted that the d,stance of projection did not apply in this case, and that for a load of timber tail liglat moat he provided. Re saw the timber waggon about three miles the other side of Meagre Long's Tannery, and the lamp was showing a very brilliant light to the front and a red light to the rear. SERIOUS ACCIDENT TO MR.

E. W. T. RICHMOND. Thrown While Cycling.

Our readers will regret to learn of a serious acci- dent which occurred to Mr E. W. T. Richmond, managing director of Messrs Richmond's Limited, on Sunday afternoon last. He was cycling in the neighbourhood of Porthecurno, Lands End, Corn- wall, when he lost control of his machine through brake failing to act, and was badly thrown head- foremost. Fortunately, Mr Richmond wag accom- panied by Dr Atkins (his brother-in-law) who ren- dered first aid and got the injured gentleman con- veyed to Rose Farm. near by. Re was found to be suffering from severe injuries to the bead and re- mained unconscious. until Tuesday evening. Four other medical men were called in in consultation, and att operation was first thought necessary but finally abandoned. The latest news is that Mr Richmond is improving.

The Firth Wire Mattress Company Glee Party.

—Picnic to Cheiter:

It may not be generally known that in connec- tion with this firm some of the employees recently originated a male voice glee party with llr E. Roberts as conductor. Although it has only been in existence about three months such progress has been made that they are already able to give a goctcl account of themselves. Practices are held, we believe each day, sufficient time being snatched out of the dinner hour to"run through"a couple of glees at the works, with one weekly evening practice in a schoolroom. The directors of the firm on hearing of the enthusiastic way in which the workmen had taken the matter up promised financial assistance in the wry of buying music, and what is more showed a kindly personal interest, in attending a rehearsal and afterwards expressed the pleasure experienced, with a sympathetic reference to the efforts put forth and enceuraging the men to perse- vere. Picnicing with Lancashire and Yorkshire singers is a sine qua non,and therefore the members, together with:their wives and young ladies decided to make an afternoon's excursion to Chester. Satur- day last was the day fixed upon, and in a special corridor saloon the party, numbering over forty, accompanied by the conductor and Nit Simms (Sec- rotary). left Warrington at 12 30, arriving at Cheater at 118. Although it poured with rain as they embarked en the train, ere Chester was reach- ed there was glorious sunshine which lasted throughout the day. Proceeding straight through Grosvenor Park to the river a journey was made up the Dee to Eaton Hall, the interior of which was greatly admired, and after a short ramble through the Park back to Eccleston, the return journey was soon accomplished and there was just sufficient time to peep into St. John's and admire the old ruins ere partaking of tea at the Commer- cial Hotel. Nit' Cnlen provided a splendid repast, at the close ef which an hour was devoted to music, several mernb-rs singing songs while the following glees were gene through :—" Here's life and health to England's King." "In a cell or cavern deep." "Comrades in Arms," " Mynheer Van Dunek," " 0 who will o'er the downs." etc. Mr Robinson, for- merly of Chester Cathedral Choir, who had been a guest during the day, kindly conducted a few of the glees and elso acting as guide through the city, Pointing ont the principal features such as " God's Providence Home," " rhe Old Crypt," " Bishop Lloyd s Palaee," the rows, courts. etc. All too soon ten o'clock arrived, at which time they had to leave, and home wee reached shortly before eleven, a thoroughly happy time having been spent,

Reference at Northwich Congrega-

tional Church.

At the close of the morning service at the Congrega- tional Church, Northwich, on Sanday,the Ray S T Miles, the pastor, said he felt sure it would be in harmeny with the feelings of those The worshipned in that church, if he referred in a few sentences only, to the lose which the district had sustained during the last few days by the death of Mr Edward Milner. The deceased was in- timately known to a number of the members of tbs.; church. They had the advantage of his co-operation, and of his help in the discharge of the more secular duties which they were called upon to do, and his un- tiring devotion and disinterested service in those de- partments would receive at the hands of the member. of thoFe authorities the tribute of their worth, and of their regard for the person whose place would in that way be very difficult to fill. He would be sorely missed. They in connection with that church could claim in an especial manner to appreciate worth wherever it was found. Mr Milner was a plain Christian citizen. He belonged to the Society of Friends, but his kindly sym- pathy had ever been shown toward a all sections of the Christian Church, whenever that church had any reason to appeal to him for sympathy and help. On sevnal cc- elisions the deceased had readily rendered help to them In that church, and his family had always shown the kindliest interest towards those who worshipped there, it might justly be staid of him thab in a quiet, unnstenratinus manner he discharged all his duties in a painstaking and conscientieua way, and the district which he had left behind, would suffer no small loss by the death of such a man.

Mr. Halton and His Defeat.

A meeting of the Great Sankey School Board was held on Tuesday afternoon in the Boardroom, when there were present Messrs Thomas Eialton (chairman), J Fulton Smith, Joseph Wright, Thomas Perks, G Clarke (headmaster) and W D Jeans, jun. (clerk). The Clerk reported that the Treasurer's account book had beeo made up to that morning. There was a balance in favour of the Board of £74 17s Sci• The Headmaster reported that the average attendance for the month was 239.2, and that fees amounted 29. 3i 4d.

_ _ • The Clerk reported that be had received the cortificate of the election of five new members of the Beard, but he thought that was a matter which should be dealt with at the next Board meeting. Mr Fulton Smith said that as this was the last meet. log of the present Board it was their duty to record their sincere and hearty thanks to the Chairman for the pleasant relationships which had existed while be had presided over their deliberations,and for the prompt and bu.inesslike way in which he had discharged his duties. To have, had such a person in the position of chairman was of great importance to the ratepayers, because owing to his business training they might rely upon him to see that their interests were not neglected and that undue expenditure was not incurred. Very few ratepayers cared to pay the rates, and they were paid with bad grace if they were heavier than was considered equitable. (near, bear). It was with much regret the members were about to lose Mr Hilton's counsel and experience. He would carry with him their best wishes, especially as the had workel together so amicably. (Hear, bear.) Mr Perks seednied the motion with pleasure and endorses all the previous speaker had said. They hal workelharinoniously together u oder Mr Halton's chair- manship and they were sorre to lose his services. He consiSered it was base ingratitude on the part of the people of Sankey not t', have re-eleeted hie after his services to the township. The Clerk, having encl-,rsed the remarks of the two previous speakers, put the motion to the meetin and it was carried. In reply, the Chairman said he had presided over the cneetiars of the Board for six or seven years,and during, the whole of that time they had done their duty and worked the schools at practic )117 speaking, no cost to the ratepayers • during the past four years the schools had been worked absolutely free. They got the highest grant and the schools paid for themselves. The only thing by which they were penaliz )1 was in having to pay off the debt for building the schoele. He had had many ups and downs' but they had worked amicably together, and he hoped their successors would act in a similar manner and do their duty on behalf of the town- shit,. Several tithes there bad been no meetings owing to the want of a quorum, bat he had only missed two meetings himself during the whole of tire time he had been connected with the Board. (Hear, hear). He was sorry he had to leave his old colleagues, bit': he hopei those who were in office w ,uld study the interests of the soheols. (Hear, hear ) Mr Halton then proposed a vote of thanks to the Vic 3 Chairman (Mr Wright), who, he said, was not only one of the oldest inhabitants but the oldest member of the Board.

EVENTS OF THE WEEK

ILLUSTRATED,

Not only will his Majesty be in the Highlands during the next few weeks, but also many other members of the Royal Family. The King, who will complete his cruise round the coast by Monday at the latest, will, of course, be at Balmoral, Queen Victoria's favourite home, where he will entertain a constant stream of personal friends and unofficial visitors, shooting and personally taking part in the deer drives, which are, with him, such a favourite branch of sport. There will, also, be deer drives at Mar Lodge, where the Duke and Duchess of Fife are already installed with their two daughters, and it is expected that both the King and the Prince of Wales will honour the first of these with their presence. The latter, with the Princess and their children, who have spent the time between the Coronation and their journey to Deeside at North Berwick, which is fast becoming a most fashionable Scottish watering-place, will this autumn for the first time possess a permanent home of his own in the Highlands at Abergeldie Castle, of which the King has just re-acquired a long lease for this

more important problem as to whether we at wise in a/lovring Continental rivals to leave us astern in the way they.are doing. The Cedric is in most respects a replica of her older- .sister the Celtic, but some changes in detail have added slightly to her displacement, which, uthen fully loaded, will be 37,870 tons. She will have accommodation for 3,000 passengers in three classes, and is expected to take her place on the Atlantic service during the autumn.

The British Academy for the Promotion of Historical, Philosophical, and Philological Studies, which has just been incorporated by Royal Charter on the recommendation of the Privy Council, to whom the vexed question was referred for solution. last January, is not the body which Matthew Arnold aimed at establishing. Certainly his great ideal has been to some extent realised, but it is grievously disappointing that pure literature should

ABERGELDIE CASTLE

THE PRINCE OF WALERE HIGHLAND HOME.

purpose. Abergeldie was held by Queen Victoria for over half a century, and stands only three miles from Balmoral. It is a picturesque old place on the banks of the Dee, and makes a most suitable Royal Residence. For many years it was used by the Duchess of Kent, the mother of the late Queen, and from 1863 to 1888 it was the Scottish home of the present King and Queen, as Prince and Princess of Wales. Since then it has been lent to the Empress Eugenie, the Duke and Duchess of Connaught, Lord and Lady Clan william, and Lord and Lady Carrington, and now it has been appointed by his Majesty to his heir in order that he may keep him at his side. It was prophesied at the time of Queen Victoria's death that Scotland would for the future be neglected by royalty, but it certainty does not look like it.

Last December it will be remembered that Sir E. Cassel, who has since been mate a member of the Privy Council upon the occasion of the Coronation, presented his Majesty with a most generous gift of £200,000, to be devoted at. the King's discretion to some national purpose. In due course it was announced that the„King had, after deliberation into which the cancer question no doubt entered, decided to apply the whole of this large sum to the erection and en- dowment of a sanatorium for the cure of tuber- culosis on the comparatively new "open air system. For generations con- sumption, to give it the popular name, has been treated by doctors on the lines of THE CURE Or CONSUMP- strictestEeClusion TIDE : DR. ARTHUR in unventilated LATHAM rooms with a warm atmos- phere. the outside

tHE SING'S PRIZE ESSAYIST.

airbeingexciudod as rigorously as poison. However, during the last decade a com- plete revolution has set in, and a totally opposite treatment has come into vogue, the sufferers from this hitherto incurable disease being kept as far as possible in the fresh air day and night, rain or shine, and being stuffed to repletion with suitable foods under medical supervision. Dr. Walther, one of the pioneers of this unorthodox treatment, opened the first sanatorium at Nordrach in the Black Forest, and so great was his medical and financial success, than since then many " Nord- rachs," as they are called out of compliment to this great German physician, have sprung up in England. Of course, the expense of such a course of treatment is very heavy to the indi- vidual, and out of the reach of the poorer classes, andlit is his Majesty's object in his decision to bring it within the horizon of as many as possible free of charge. As a preliminary step to this national foundation it was decided to offer a prize of 4500 and others of lesser amounts to the contributors of the best essay on the subject of the disposal of the £200,000 for this purpose, together with architectural plans for a suitable sanatorium. Many essays were submitted, and the-judges' final award of the King's prize fell to Dr. Arthur Latham, a young chest specialist in London. He is only thirty-four years of age, but a very rising man in his profession. He is the son of the well- known Cambridge professor, Dr. Latham, and is an M.D. of Cambridge,, which is both his birth- place and his alma meter. Just over two years ago he was elected on to the staff of the Brompton Hospital for Diseases of the Chest, as one of the assistant physicians to out patients. The second prize fell to Dr. Wethered, of the same hospital and of the Middlesex Hospital, who is a few years older, and a man of considerable mark in his pro- fession. it is a great feather in the cap of these two young doctors, and the best possible, yet perfectly legitimate advertisement.

The latest German liners are faster than ours, but we continue to hold the record for size. Our rivals in the Fatherland go in fcr expresses. whereas it appears we prefer to sacrifice speed to cargo- carrying capacity. On August 12th, in the presence of the German Emperor, the Kai.frer TVilkelni IL, the fourth high-sped liner built in rapid succession for the Norddeutscher Lloyd Company was launched, and she is expected

to make the 'Atlantic passage in a shorter time than any of her three elder sisters, the Kaiser Wilhelm der Grosse, Deettxchland, and Xronprinz Wilhelm, all of which far excel in speed any British. owned steamer. Not only is she faster, but she is also longer than either the Oceank! or the Celtic. but when it comes to registered tonnage the latter has the advantage by nearly a thousand tons, and the new White Star leviathan, the Cedric, outmeasures her by a round thousand, the figures being Kaiser 'Wilhelm 11. 20,000, Celtic 20.904. and Cedric 21.000. The Cedric, which was launched at Belfast a few days ago—the first ve.ssel to be built for the Atlantic shipping combination—is there- fore the largest ship ationt, and whether this is a prouder title than that of the fastest ocean express iv a question which must be left for the shipping experts to decide. along with the even

SKETCH:

AT THE DUBLIN HOBSS SHOW.

opportunity for laying in new horses for the approaching season. Thus it is that each year Dublin is crowded out with visitors, and the hotels for once have the pleasure of turning .away ap- plicants and charging fancy prices owing to the competition. tt is the one time of the year when the Irish country families flock up to Dublin, even those which cannot afford to do the season, as they feel that they must do the Horse Show, and consequently come up year after year for a week of business and pleasure combined. This year a noticeable feature is the fact of the interregnum at the Castle, as Lord Cadogan has already vacated the post of Lord-Lieutenant, while Lord Dudley does not officially enter into residence until the end of the month. As a rule the Castle with the Lord-Lieutenant and his party plays a semi-royal part in the proceedings, and is the scene of evening receptions and enterthinmentg, hat this year the Show is something like Hamlet without a Prince of Denmark, so far as the social side is concerned, with the Castle empty. The state of Ireland may be very disturbed, but in Dublin all is harmony.

have been given the cold shoulder, and the explana- tion of the promoters of the scheme, that to select representatives of poetry, fiction, and belles-lettres would be difficult, if not impossible, is as uncon- vincing as it is unsatisfactory. However, in spite of the regrettable omission, the new British Academy will have a wide field in setting a standard both of matter and manner in those studies over which it is to preside, for the Royal Charter states that "the Academy aims at the promotion of the study of moral and political sciences, including history, philosophy, law, politics and economies, azchieoloiry, and philology" —truly a formidable list! Of tin forty-nine eminent men who are included in the Charter as the first "Fellows" the names of Mr. Balfour, philosopher and orator, the Earl of Rosebery, historian, essayist, and orator, Mr. John Morley, philcoopher and critic, Dr. Murray, philogist. and. Sir William Anson, Warden of All &sure College, Oxford, stand out prominently. The remaining forty-four are all distingmished men in their re- spective spheres, but some surprise has been occa- sioned by the fact that in the long list the name of Mr. Herbert Spencer does not appear.

Two interesting and illustrious visitors have taken their departure during the last few days —King Lewanika of Barotseland, and 'another monarch of infinitely more importaace, especially in his own estimation, to wit his Imperial Majesty the Shah of Persia. The former sailed for South Africa on Saturday, and the latter crossed to Calais on Monday. Both must have carried away with them very agreeable im- pressions of England and English howi- tality, for both have had a most cordial welcome in this country, and everything that could possibly be done to make their visit enjoyable has been done— well. The Shah's sojourn amongst us was but brief, but his Majesty of Barotseland has spent a couple wso SAILED FOR rAnorsiu.situ of months in this OE SATURDAY. country, and during that time has managed

KING LEWANIKA.

to make • himself extremely popular everywhere. An extraordinary amount of interest in all his doings and some of his irayings has been manifested, and for weeks he has been the most paragraphed man in the United Kingdom with the pos.sible exception of Mr. Seddon. There are likely to be many startling innovations introduced into Barotseland when King Lewanik3 reaches home, and it is to be hoped that Col. Harding will furnish us with an account of them. It would be entertaining reading.

Once more the Dublin Horse Show has come— the greatest and most popular event of the year in the Irish Capital, when all political animosities are buried, and Orangeman and Home Ruler not only fraternise with each other, but receive the visitors from England with open arms and try in true horse-breeders' fashion to get the better of them in a deal: The annual Horse Show at Dublin has various features with its many classes and types of horses mid its famous jumping competitipns, to all of which valuable prizes are attached. But these are not the only facts which attract breeders and dealers, as purchasers from all over the United Kingdom flock to the Irish capital, especially hunting men and their women folks who find it an excellent market for recruiting their studs and a capital

THE by-election at Salutary Chastening. Sevenoaks has been re- ceived with a surprising candour by the chief organs of the Government in tle London Press. There has been no attempt to juggle with the figures, and none to disguise the real cause of a surprising change of opinion. It

matters very little that the seat still belongs to

the Government. One vote more or less cannot affect so vast a majority, evert when, as the " Morning Post" puts it, it is suffering from "fatty degeneration." What is important is that this election, following hard on the heels of Bury and North Leeds, goes to prove that the opinion of the country has suffered a revolution since 1900. When the rural elector in Kent abandons himself to the same flowing tide as the townsman of Lan- cashire and Yorkshire, it is clear enough that Mr. BALFOUR'S majority is morally gone. A General Election would reduce the Unionist party to the most modest proportions. At North Leeds there was a change of some votes ; at Sevenoaks though the Tory majority still survives, the change was actually of 4,000 votes. At this rate one could count on the fingers of one hand the number of really safe seats among those contested in 1900. But what will come of it all ? The " Standard" admits that "a change" is taking place throughout the country—which, indeed, seems to be obvious. The "Morning Post," true blue Tory though it is, rejoices in the "salutary chastening" to which the Ministry is being sub- jected. The " Daily Mail," indignant with Mr. BALFOUR'S " nepotism," with the bungling of the War Office, and the " inefficiency" of most of the Ministers, discovers that this Government,elected on an issue :which no longer exists, is in a thoroughly "false position." It is nice to have all these frank admissions. But neither candour nor by-elections will defeat the Education Bill. Clause 7 has been carried, and even if. Mr. BALFOUR saw the wisdom of a compromise it is too late for him to go back on point which he has maintained with such obstinacy. He might, of course, withdraw the Bill altogether; but having weathered the storm in the House of Commons, such as it was, he is hardly likely to accept defeat unless he has reason to fear the most serious consequences in the country. In face of a situation such as this, the resolution of the Leeds Free Church Council in favour of a militant policy is opportune. The suggestion is that a list should be drawn up without delay of all Nonconformists thoughout the country who are prepared to refuse to pay a rate for the support of clerical schools. OUR hopes that this extreme defiance may not be necessary, but the way to avert it is to show some organised determination before it be too late. If some thousands of Free Churchmen would sign a solemn league and covenant to pay no rates under Mr. BALFOUR'S Bill—still better, if a number of town and country, and even parish councils,would followhe example of Carnarvonshire, by binding themselves not to administer the Act if it passes in its present form—its fate would be sealed. The stake is quite big enough. Free/Churchmen cannot quietly allow the High Church to become for all time the only instructor of youth in some 8,000 parishes. Liberals cannot calmly surrender the doctrine that all taxation implies representation.

The Sequels of Peace.

THE cape Parlilment has met at last, Martial

Law is at an end, and the affairs of the Colony seem to be going smoothly. The faction of ascendency which calls itself "loyalist "—meaning by that word, as one of its leaders explained at a recent public meeting, that it is "loyal" to Lord MILER—has quite failed to *wreck the Constitution of the Colony, but has succeeded in splitting the so-called " Progressive " or Rhodeeian party. It has failed to dislodge Sir

", • • ' • ,

Gtoito ON SPRIG from power, but it has brough about a union of the Moderates of both races Fort the defence of the liberties of their common country. And that is the best thing that could have happened for South Africa. So long as party distinctions were also racial divisions there could be no prospect of an enduring peaco. There is to be a Commission to redress the gross grievances of Martial Law. On the other hand, even the extreme members of the Bond have made no difficulty about passing the necessary acts of indemnity. The ingoes, on the other hand, have no chance of carrying their Coercion Bill. The "Times," of course, is doing its best to discredit Sir GORDON SPRIGG because he owes his position to the Dutch vote. But that transparent party cry will deceive no one. The co-operation of the two races offers the only pros- pect of peace, and the man who can bring it about deserves our warmest congratulations. What South Africa needs is a strong party devoted to the cause of self-government. No one is offended when Sir WILFRID LAURIER talks of Canada as an " independent " nation. Why should the Bond be an object of suspicion when it cherishes a sun i ;ar ideal? It is not a disloyal body. Mr. RHODES worked with it before the Jame- son Raid, as Sir GORDON SPRIGG is doing now. It was the Bond which first proposed to make sn annual contribution to the Imperial , Navy, It is hostile not to Empire, but to that meddlesome spirit of Imperialism which seeks to govern from Downing-street in the interests of one race. The real enemy of the Empire in South Africa is that alien party of ascendency within it, the capitalists whose headquarters are in Paris and Park-lane, and the floating population which fills the ports and the mining centres, with no thought of making the country its permanent home. It is this party which made the Jameson Raid, provoked the war, and is now seeking to destroy the Constitution of the Cape. It is to South Africa what the " garris*n " is to Ireland. For the moment the danger in the Cape is over. The next battle will be in the new territories, where Lord MILN PM is proposing to acquire Boer farms by compulsory power in order to settle British immigrants upon them. If that plan succeeds the fair hope of conciliation will be wrecked.

THE gigantic finan- "Municipal Socialism." cial ring which is

rapidly acquiring something very like a monopoly of electrical enter- prise in Great Britain has opened an interesting campaign against "municipal socialism " in the columns of the "Times," ever the organ of monopoly and privilege. The word has a danger- ous ring about it, and it is easy to excite prejudice by talking of communism and its perils. But what, after all, is it which a community does when

it decides to provide its own tramways,its own gas, or its own water P It simply decides that, as these are commodities which cannot very well be supplied by free competition, it is unsafe to allow a monopoly to make its profits unchecked at the expense of the public. If your baker overcharges you, you have only to transfer your custom to his rival. But there is no room in one town for com- peting tramways or rival gas companies. These things are natural monopolies. The ordinary economic rules of supply and demand under a sys- tem of free competition cease to apply in their , case. Hence the growing tendency on the part of all modern communities to provide these things for the benefit of the people, under direct public control. The principle is now so firmly rooted that it needs no defence. Its applications will grow precisely as the parallel tendency towards "trusts "grows. Competition is one advantage which private enterprise has over State or municipal enterprise. The moment competition becomes inoperative a case for public control can be made out. With our great railway. talking ,of amalgamation, with an Atlantic trust " cornering " our shipping, with another ring seizing our electrical industries, the sphere of possible public enterprise, indefinitely extends itself. We cannot watch these developments too closely. America is finding out too late what dangers are associated with its gigantic trusts. Mr BRYAN and the Democratic party have long been conducting a campaign against them. They failed, but so crying is the danger that Mr ROOSE- vELT, Republican though he is, has at last come forward with a sweeping scheme for establishing a national system of c'ontrol over these selfish monopolies which have grown up in the shelter of a protective tariff that excludes all foreign com- petition. We can only watch and learn. So long as we retain our Free Trade uninfringed, matters are hardly likely to come to so desperate a pass with us. But we are by no means safe.

We hope that British working men read the correspondence from South Africa. It will open the eyes of some who were blind to the true facts of the war. We hope, too, that they will not fall into the trap laid for them by "assisted emigra- tion." As" black" labour cannot be obtained in sufficient quantities for the mines," white" labour must be obtained. If there is any difficulty about this, "the Government must take up the cause" by encouraging immigration into South Africa; and " the cost will be defrayed by the public funds." So writes the Johannesburg correspondent of the "Standard." Life for working men in this coun- try is, we know, not "all beer and skittles " ; bat it is not yet reduced to the level of the Rind, and never will be if the working classes are only true to themselves. As to the cool suggestion that the British taxpayers should pay the cost of sending labour outto Johannesburg, Mr Ritchie will find it difficult to pass any Budget with such an item in it.

The figures relating to the exports of manufac- tured goods, which that useful paper Commercial Intelligence has recently published, ought;to give pause to the pessimistic protectionists, who are for ever crying "Ichabod ! " over the trade of this country. While it cannot be denied that other countries, and especially Germany, have increased. their exports at a greater ratio than Great Britian, yet we still hold a large and abolute supremacy. Here are the figures: (1) The United Kingdom .- £230,000,000 (2) The German Empire 150,000,000 (3) France... 85,000,000 (4) The United States 80,000,000 If we were to adopt the egregious Mr. Seddon's advice, and fight these protectionist countries with their own weapons, the relative positions of the four above-named nations would soon be changed. It is because we are a Free-trade country, and buy in the cheapest and sell in the dearest markets that we can find, that we are able to export more of our manufactures than the others. Directly we began to tax imports from other countries, they would increase their duties on our exports, which would become dearer, and therefore we should sell less of them. This is the very ABC of the Free Trade on which our commercial supremacy is based, and it is strange that the 1.39800 should still be so difficult for some people to learn.

The Sevenoaks election result will be as inspiring to Liberals and Nonconformists generally as the great victories at Bury and Leeds. It is true that

On Saturday Mrs Grubb, wife of Walter Grubb White-street, Runcorn, was found to be very ill and suffering severe internal pain. It transpired that she had swallowed a quantity of ammonia, and an emetic of salt and water was administered with successful effect. Dr McLennan was called in. She is recovering slowly. by JOHN'S BAND OF HOPE.

The weekly Session of the Runcorn Forward Lodge 2444, was held on Monday evening last, being opened in due form by Bro Anders, C.T. The usual order of business being proceeded with, the election of officers for the quarter was taken, resulting in the return of the principal officer as per last quarter Sister Patrick taking V.T. A short musical pro- gramme was then gone through, and an enjoyable and inspiring Session brought to a close. The Lodge is expecting to take a more aggressive attitude during the winter months, and is expect- ing much assistance from prominent speakers. RUNCORN WOMAN'S DEATEI UNDER CLHOROPORM. Mr T E Sampsom,the Liverpool Coroner, on Wed- nesday held an inquiry concerning the death of Mary Murphy (34), the wife of Thomas Murphy, a chemical labourer,who lived in Ivy-street, Runcorn. Deceased suffered from a throat complaint, and it was decided that an operation should be performed at the Liverpool Royal Infirmary. Chloroform was administered, the deceased appearinz a perfectly fit subject, but the operation was not completed when a t:hange took place in her condition, and she died from heart failure. Medical testimony showed that the chloroform was skilfully administered,and the jury, in returning a verdict in accordance with this evidence, expressed the opinion that the arrangements for the operation had been very properly carried out. ST. HELENS TRADERS AND THE CO-OPERATIVE STORES. INTEREST INCREASING.

(CONTINUED 1RO:11 PAGE 3.

The announcement made at the meeting of the St Helens and District Traders' Defence Association, that the president of the St Helens ludustri Al Co-operative Society had withdrawn from the cooperative society has given gfeat satisfaction to the traders of the town, who feAl that many other influential gentlemen may now sever their connection with the s;ores. Wednesday night's meeting was large and enthusiastic and there was no doubt that the tradesmen were very well satisfied with the progress reported. The interest taken in the movement is every (14y increasing, and the I chairman and secretary of the Traders' Association are really bombarded with letters from other towns inquir- ing as to the progress of the fight in St Helens, seeking for information as to the methods of procedure, and even offering monetary assistance in the struggle. With the shop assistants and the lady tracers joining hands with the traders, it is felt a strong force has been got together to reduce the influence of the stores.

THE EXAMINER FRIDA V AUGUST 29. 1902

the Conservative has been again returned, but what gratification can there be in a victory gained only by a majority of 891 against a margin of no less than 4,812 votes at the last election? If ever there was a moral victory this is one, and the blow to the self confidence of the Government could hardly have been more deadly. Mr. Balfour was quite hurt that Liberals should venture to contest this " safe " Conservative seat, while at the same time pathetically anxious that the voters of Sevenoaks would not desert him. Mr. Chamberlain was, no doubt, little less anxious, but he characteristically tried to bluff the elector- ate with the astonishing declaration that the Education Bill would "give complete control of secular education." To whom? has evidently been the question asked and answered by the Seven- oaks voters. Neither the Premier nor the Colonial Secretary can have felt very happy on Friday when the news came of the startling reversal of public opinion in yet another constituency. The elec- tion was fought on the home policy of the Govern- ment as exemplified by the Corn Tax and the Education Bill, and the result can be considered in no other light than as another sweeping con- demnation by the public of the measures the party in power persist in forcing upon the country. At this rate the Government will soon be glad to either stuff thelEducation Bill in some obscure corner with the pious prayer that it Imay be forgotten, or get out of office!
