WATER POLO.

Warrington Defeated by Wigan.

Warrington played their return league snatch with Wigan at the Corporation Baths, Legh-streec, on Tues- day evening, in tile presence of a large attendance of spectators. Mr McDonald,of Liverpool,was the referee, and the teams entered the bath as follows :—Warring- ton : Robertson, goal ; Boscow and W Ashton, backs ; C Ashton, half-backs ; J Peers, A Ashton, and W Sutton, forwards. Wigan : 0 F Smith, goal ; F Bearsley and W Magraw, backs; Saunders, half-back ; U Morrison, T B Swift and T Matthews, forwards. Warrington de- fended the deep end, and Roberton was very soon called upon to save. A good move by Peers took play to the other end and C Ashton made a good attempt at goal. Smith, however, was on the alert. The visitors now attacked with vigour and Matthews registered the first goal. Play continued in the Warrington quarters, and Matthews again scored. Kearsley sent in a good shot but Robertson saved. Swift eventually got the ball and notched the visitors' third goal, and soon after wards Matthews scored from close quarters. The Warrington goal was very hotly assailed and Robertson warded off three shots in quick succession Half time arrived with the score, Wigan four goals, War. rington nil. In the second half play was of a more even nature, both ends being visited in turn. Saunders, however, managed to put the bell through agam for Wigan. W Ashtora made a capital attempt, but Smith was equal to the emergency. When the whistle sounded "time," Wigan were the victors by live goals to nil.

RUNCORN NOTES.

tBYA CoRRESPONDENT]._

There was a lengthy discussion at the Board of Guardians on Tuesday re a sanatorium for con- sumptives. The Board, as a body appear to be in favour of the erecting of such a building but do not care to commit themselves to the necessary ex- penditure. The building of such places would meet a crying want and it is to be trusted that ere long, a sanatorium will be erected in the neighbourbold.

The opposition of the Free Churchmen to the proselytizing Education Bill is slowly but none the less surely growing. The National Free Church Council is leading the van in the agitation against the measure. During the last week the Rev T Law, organising secretary, forwarded to the eight hundred Free Church Councils throughout the country. a circular letter asking the opinion of the Councils concerning the question of the non. payment of the rates should the bill become law The action of these councils will, of course, regu- late the future action of the 'iCxecutive in its organ- ised active or passive resistance when the demand note is left with the hauseholde,. In common I with the other councils Runcorn will have to decide a question which is one of the most momentous in the history of education. The action of the councils, as a body, is fraught with consequences BO far reaching that many timid Nonconformists may hesitate to toe the line with such men as Dr Clifford, •Dr Parker, Rev 4Hugh Price Hughes, Dr Robertson Nicholl, Dr Townsend (President of the National Council), Professor Niassie, M. A, Rev Silvester Horne, M A, and other leading gentlemen who advocate the policy of non-payment of that proportion of the rate which would have to be paid to the Voluntary Schools if the Bill be passed.

The present movemept is not a time "to sicken I and to swoon" at con%equences. If the Bill be wrong in principle then it must be right to oppose it from the beginning to the end—no matter how I bitter that end may be. If the Nonconformists,who are certainly fighting strenuously simply content I themselves with threats till the last ditch be passed I and then let their clamour die away when the de- mand note is presented little wonder can there be 1 that among churchmen there is a feeling akin to contempt for their opponents. Fearlessly the Runcorn Free Church Council will have to decide whether resistance to the payment of this new church rata is to be made or not, In spite of many ugly rumours that the Council will sullenly yield to the Government pressure after the last lap it cannot be conceived that the Council will bend the knee so humbly. There is a time when revolt is far more sacred and constitutional that, passive submissiveness. Such a time was in the days of Cromwell who refused to pay rates levied by the King acting on the advice of his bishops. Such a time it is now for Free Church- men to refuse payment of rates to a Government who act on the advice of their Clerical advnates. It is to he sincerely trusted that the Runcorn Coun- cil will follow in the wake of the Leeds Council who by eighty votes to one, determined to resist,at all costs, payment of this new sacerdotal impost.

RUNCORN BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

On Tuesday the fortnightly meeting of this Board was held at Dutton WorkhouEe. Mr J Scokes presided, and there were also present :-Issr a it Norman (vice- chairman), S Taylor, T Reynolds, G F Ashton (clerk), A Hulse (assistant clerk), J Cunnah (master), T Cooke, W De Prez, T Riley (relieving officers).

===== 0002601_19020829_art0073.txt | included=true | included =====
CON3I:7IIPTI VE SANAT:3RIIIM.

Mr Ashton, the clerk, suhmitted a report of the Con- ference with reference to the Consumptive Sanatoria, held at Crewe, and explained that the County CDUCICiI desired to be informed whether Oa 3 following resolution met with the approval of the Board of Guardians and the Rural Council :—" That in the opinion of this Con- ference, but without in any way committing the several authorities represented, it is desirable that a sanatorium or sanatoria should be established in the County for the treatment of consumption, and that the County Council should take tke necessary steps under the Isolation Hospitals Act, with a view to an order being made including all the sanitary districts in the county a hos- pital district, for the establisment of such sanatorium or sanatoria, and at the same time obtain the consent of the Local Governmeot Board to include " phthisis " as an infectious disease within the meaning of the Infec- tious Diseases Notificatioo Act,

Mr Norman, who was present at the Conference, said thee was an emphatic feeling that they should not com- mit themselves.

The Clerk said the County Council considered they could not provide a sanatorium unless consumption was made a notifiatie disease. The County Council had written stating that subsequent to the hilding of the Conference the Local Government Board had expressed the opinion that it was competent for the County Council under section 26 of the Isolation Hospit di Act to apply the provisions of that act to any disease other than th.se specified without a notification of such cases being ren- dered compulsory. If the Biard tnouetit it undesirable that the disease should be made notifiable they could attach it to the resolution.

Mr Norman said there seemed to be a strong feeling against notification at the Conference but on receiving the emphatic opinion that unless the diseas was notifi- able the County Council could not move in the mater the meeting was practically unanimous. Replying to Mr. Baxter, the Clerk said the Counts, Council acted on a resolution passed at a meeting of delegates from various Boards of Guardians at Birken- head. It was thought that the matter should be taken up in a broader manner than was possible with indi- vidual Boards. Tee County Council were quite willing to take up toe question if the County required a sanatorium.

Mr Norman said the original idea was simply a sana- torium for dealing with the disease. The question of making the disease notifiable was incotrd at Crewe. The question of notification was distiuct from that of estab- lishing sanatoria. M. Woodward said the County Council Health Com- mittee to whom the matter was referred took it up with energy and enthusiasm. It was not necessary that the disease should be made notifiable before the sanatorium was established. He had not been able to satisfy. himself that the alleviation of sufferers from that disease was permanent. Instances had been provided him by medical men where patients who had been returned home as cured had died in a comparatively short time. Pulmonary disease was the thing they wanted to deal with, and before they knew the requirements of the County or ny given district for sanatoria .ithey : should have a division made between the pulmooary disease, and the other forms of tuber- cular disease. Whilst he favoured measures being taken for the alleviation of that dr..eadfu disease, they ought not to give their adherence to any large scheme. Admiral Parry said he would not obj.ct to sanatoria in the county for the workhouses, but was opposed to anything else. Soon they would want Faaatoria for hi everytng. _ _ . . .

- . The Chairman took it that they as a Board could orly deal with paupers. They had very few cases. Admiral Parry said there were 68 cases of phthisis in the unions of the county which would fill an ordinary hospital. Linaker said he had been told by doctors who were specialists on the subject that although they could afford relief they did not believe in a permanent cure. Mr Wright could not understand that Manchester would have taken the matter up if sanatoria had not I proved effective. There were about 600 deaths in I Cheshire alone from the disease, and if they could do anything to relieve the sufferers they ought to do so. The Clerk said the meeting was a joint one,and conse- quently the sanatorium would not be for the use of paupers, but for the county. Mr Norman adoonted that the County Council should move cautiously in the matter, and deal with it more in an experimental way than anythioq else. Mr Talbot thought there was not sufficient jnetification for such a large expenditure. Until those sanatoria had been proved they ought not to take any action. Mr Woodward moved "That in the opinion ef this Board of Guardians the County Ciuncil should take some steps for the establishment of a sanatorium for the treatment of pulmonary phthisis, but that the day had not arrived when any large expenditure should be incurred until the efficiency of the treatment had been proved." Mr Williams argued that it had been proved. lie seconded the motion.

Mr Norman supported. and it was approved,

Mr W E Enollys, assistant srcretary of the Local Government Board transmitted the following report received from the Commissioners in Lunsoy of a visit to the Workhouse on tho 24th ult., by Mr Urmson, one of the Visiting Commissioners :—" I found at my visit to-day to this (the Dutton) Workhouse only four female inmates classed u inmates. They were all so classed at my colleague's visit in 1900, and all remain suitable cases to remain in the house. I found them all at work in the laundry, They were neatly dressed,: looked well, and without exception spoke well of their treatment. The bathing arrangements, to which exception was taken at the visit in 1900, have been im- proved in the sense that it now taken place in the Receiv- ing instead of the Tramp Wards. It seems a pity that

arrangements cannot be made for the imbeciles to go out tor occasional walks in the country to relieve the mono- tony of their lives. The rooms ware very clean and so was the bedding that I examined. All the imbeciles sleep in ene dormitory, which has a second exit to r use in case of fire. There is no record of the use of mechanical restraint.

The seventeenth annual exhibition of the Newton-1e• Willows, Earlestown and District Horticultural Society was held on Saturday in a field kindly lent by Mr John Randall, of Newton Nurseries. EAR LESTOWN & NEWTON.

GOOD TEMPLARY.

The weekiy meeting of the I 0 G T wee held on Monday night at the Co-operative Hall,Earle.street, tile; programme being a musical evening open to the public, of which a goud attendance of visitors took advantage. Opening ode sung together, phono- graph selections by Bro Greening, sung by Sister Williamson, reading by Bro Williamson, two songs by Mies Stronach, recitation by Bro J Warburton, Miss Tombs kindly presided at the piano, Bro Hatton, C T, being chairman. COAL STEALING AT ST. HELENS. . . .

At St Helens on Monday, two girls named Annie M'Cann and Louisa Wersley,of Warrington Old-road were brought up on a charge of stealing coal from the White Cross Colliery. Police- constable Adams saw the girls taking the coal from the colliery screen. M'Cann's mother said the girl had been there several times before like hundreds of others, and it was very queer the others could not be caught. Worsley's mother admitted that she knew her daughter had gone out to pick coal. The bench imposed a fine of 5s in each case. The Chief Constable stated that on the next occasion he would summon the parents in- stead of the children: BRUTAL ATTACK ON A ST. HELENS CONSTABLE, At St Helens on Monday, a man named Thomas Casey, of no fixed residence, was charged wiz,h assaulting Police-Constable Perry, who bore evi- dence of having been very badly used, his right eye being blackened and his band Dlastered up. The constable stated that on Saturday night there was a disturbance in Liverpool-road, and Police-constable Hurst and he went to quell it. The prisoner rushed behind him and struck him a violent blow on the eye knocking him down, While on the ground the prisoner kicked him on the head, and was making another running kick at his head, when he put up his hand to save him- self. His hand was kicked and almost burst. In reply to the Chief Constable, the officer stated that be bad never spoken to the prisoner, and was not going to arrest him. He came up deliberately and struck him.—The prisoner said he was very sorry for what had happened, but he was very drunk The Chief Constable said prisoner had been up fifteen times before for various offences, including burglary.--Sentence of three months' hard labour • was passed.

EMMEN

LATEST NEWS.

The Channel Swim.

Holbein's Hard Luck.

Both Holbein and Holmes have failed in their attempts to swim the English Chatmet. Holbein was in the sea over 22 hours, but towards the last his progress was very slow,and at hall-past one in the afternoon,althougla I he was only three-quarters of a mile from Dover, it was decided that be was incapable of completing the dis- tance. Holmes started from Dover at haq-past five in the morning, and snide good headway for about four hours, when he complained of sea-sickness and of being , almost blinded by the reflection of the sun on the water. Success under these conditions being impossible, the effort was abandoned.

The Crops. During Friday night there was five hours' heavy dewnpour of rain in Cheshire, and the farmers are becoming gloomily apprehensive cencerning.the harvest. On Friqay many acrc,s of oats were cut, and on Saturday morning were terribly soaked and battered. Oats are at least three weeks and wheat fire weeks late. The absence of sun has caused uneven ripening, and t,le rains threaten to be serious. The corn crops are heavy, with goad straw, if they can be ripened. Roots are late, but satis- factory. Barley is poor. Hay generally has been splendidly got, but some remains, And is spoiling. The Cheshire fruit crop is a failure. Messrs. Joseph Crosfield's and Sons Limited

Fitting Shop. The annual picnic ia connection with ths above took place on Saturday last, August 23rd when a visit was paid to Chester, leaving Bank Quay Station at 12,24 p.m. arriving at Chester at 12.50 p.m. There were conveyances in readiness to con- vey the party through Alford to Fdrndon, where tea was served at the Raven Hotel. After a stay of two hours in this pretty village, the party then proceeded over the Dee into Wales through Holt, Gresford, and Rossitt, arciving in Chester at S. p.m. the train leaving Chester at 9 p.m. Warrington was reached at 950 p.m. There was a consenu4 of opinian that a more enjoyable picnic had yet to be recorded in connection with the above.

A lad named Willi. Husband was standing on the stonework near Ouse Bridge, York on Wednesday even- ing, when he overbalaricel and fell into deep water and sank at once. SINGULAR CASE AT WARRINGTON. -- Revelations at the County Court.

Allegations Against Trustees. Mr Crompton (Messrs Browne and Crompton) made some singular revelations at the Warrington County Court on Thursday, last week, which led Judge Bowen Rowlands to remark that if the facts as ere as stated they constituted a gross fraud. The allegations ware made during the hearing of a case to whieu Mr Marks representing Mr Wolfe Jacksonononey-lander of Cheater souzht to obtain a judgment for £ls3s 6d against Philip Robinson, a porter in the employ of the Cheshire Lines Committee at Warrington Central Statios, and Frederick Robinson, a news agent of Orford-lane. Mr. Crompton, said he was not appearing profession- ally in tbe case, but wished to say on behalf of the defendants that some time ago judgment was obtained against them for the sum of £lO. That judgment was gazetted in the usual way and shortly afterwards a Birmingham firm sent a circular letter to the defendants saying that as they were in monetary difficulties they were willing to assist them. A representatire came to Warrington and told defendants that if they wauld sign a paper everything would be all r4zht. He got defend- ants to execute a document, which afterwards was discovered to be a deed of assignment of all their pro- perty for the benefit of their creditors. The trustee then said he would not realize their estate, provided they would pay 121 a week for the purpose of purchas- ing the estate back from/the trusters. The amount so realized was afterwards to be distributed among the creditors wha assented. The sum of £l6 was paid at the rate of 12a a week,and then defendants began to fell into arrear. An auctioneer was sent dowa from Birming- ham,lwho said he was authorized by the trustee to sell, and he did so, His (Mr Crompton's) firm lad written several letters to the firm in Birmingham, but had been unable to get any satisfactory information regarding the matter. With respeot to the present claim the defend- ants did not dispute it. They borrowed £ll 103 in the first instance and had repaid £8 17a 6d. The claim of £l5 3s 6d was for interest and balance due. The wife uf Philip Robinson was called, and said Mr Crompton's statement was quite true. Has Honour remarked that there was no blame attached to Mr Jackson, and continued : That is the way of assisting people out of debt, is it ? Did they advance you any money ? Mrs Robinson . No your honour. We never had a penny from them. Mr M•rlts said they knew nothing of the deed of assignment. Mrs Robinson : The representative who came from Birmingham took a list of creditors and said they would write to them.

Mr Marks : We hays never heard from them.

His Honour (to Mrs Robinson) : I hope you won't sign anything again without asking somebody's advice about it. That is a curious way of assisting creditors. It is a very shocking thing. rho greatest publicity should he given to such eases. His Honour (to Mr Marks) : I will ask you to with- draw the summons. I cannot make a commitment.

Mr Crompton : I will give Mr Mark the name of the trustees.

His Honour (to Mr Marks) : I hope you will make inquiry. Upon the facts as stated it is a gross fraud. It is sometimes very difficult to dial with these people. Sometimes there is no firm or fixed place of business. I don't know whether it is so in this case.

Mr Crompton : their replies to our letter have not been satisfactory. His Honour : It is a case that demands the fullest investigation. If this sort of thing exists no judge will make a commitment against the defendant. ... am glad it has been brought before the Court. Mr Unsworth (s-elicitor) pointed out that it was open to any creditor to tske Elation against the trustee for a statement of account.

Mr Cr3ropton : We have not be^n able to get hold of a creditor.

His Honour (to Mr Marks) This is well worth your while considering becauseivou will not be able to get a cotrunitrnent in cases where this has taken place. The case was withdrawn, Mr Marks stating that he would make inquiries.

DEATH OF MR. EDWARD MILNER. -- Many in Warrington and district will regret to hear of the death of Mr Edward Milner, who passed away in his 65th year at his residence, Hartford Maaor,Northwich on:Thursday last week. He had been confined to bed for a month, suffering from a complication of diseases. Despite the greatest care and attention he breathed his last shortly after nine o'clock on Thursday morning. He leaves a widow and three children.

Mr Milner was a Warrington citizen and prior to his removal to Hartford Manor be took an active interest in local affairs. He was the eldest son of the late Mr William Edward Miiner, who many years ago carried on the business at a tailor and draper at Market Gate, and resided at Fir Grove, Latchford. There was a family of six children,viz : Mr Edward Miler, who died on Thursdny ; Mr Arthur Milner, who at the time of his death some years ago was manager of Parr's Beak, Chester; Mr Walter Milner, who is still actively connected with the Whitecrose Wire and Iron Company Limited ; Miss Milner; Mrs William Long, of Grappenball ; and Mrs 3 M 11 Todhunter, of Lymm: The late Mr Edward Milner resided at Springfield House in Sankey-street. From 1875 to November 1887, he was a member of the Warrington Town Council, and represented north-east ward. As deputy chairman of the Sanitary Committee he did much useful work in improving the sanitary cm dition of the town. He also sat on the Museum- Committee and was a keen ornithologist. He took a great interest in education and became a trustee of the Grammar Scheel and a manager of the People's CollegelSchool For a long time he was sec- retary for the sehool when it was situated in Newtcn-street, where the Central Station now stand. In 1880 he was appointed a justice of tke peace for the borough by the Chancellor of the Duchy of Lancaster. He was a member of the Society of Friends up to the time of his death, whilst in politics he was a sound Liberal. He was a successful commercial man and 30 years ago wee a salt proprietor at Mars- ton, near Northwich. He was also the originator of the Sankey White Lead Works, in which he was associated with Mr Dale. When the firm of Messre Brunner, Mond, and Co, Limited, was launched, Mr Milner was appointed a director, and continued un- til his death to be one of the chiefs of the VI inning- ton works. His interest is public affairs continued and he became a member of the Salt Compensation Board, a representative of the River Weaver Trust, and a member of the Cheshire County Council. _ .

, He left Warrington for Hartford Manor, about 15 years ago when he married a Miss Stromeyer. Since his removal from Warrington his interest in War- rington • affairs gradually deelined and the Sankey White Lead Works passed into the hands of the present company. A Warrington friend of the late Mr Milner has furnished us with some pleasant recollections of a little trip he bad in company with Mr Milner in the Easter time of 1881. It was at the kind invitation of Mr Milner, he writes, that I accompanied Mr Milner on a brief holiday in the Lake district. We left Warrington at 9.50 on Thursday, the 14th April, 1881 and went on to Tebay, then on to Kirkby Stephen and thence to Kirkby Thorpe. From here we went to Millburn and stayed the night, enjoying to the full the I quiet solitude of the district and the bracing at- mosphere. At night we were disturbed by the intrusion of a jackdaw which effected an entrance by the chimney and greatly alarmed us for a time. Next day was Good Friday and we enjoyed:a beauti- ful walk to the Crossfell and in passing down the valley I well recollect Mr Milner making the fol- lowing observation 'How beautiful these moun- tains are, and with what mighty majesty they stand,and what poor little things we are when we come to look at them!" On the following day we drove together from Millburn to Kirkby Thorpe ' and Temple Sowerby, afterwards to Penrith and thence to Keswick. On Easter Monday we left Keswick and drove to the vale of St, Joha to Thirlespot where we made a geological survey in connection with the lead mines there. From here we drove to Grasmere, and on to Rydal and Amble. side visiting en route the Stock Gbyl force. We then took a cat to Windermere where we caught a train for Warrington. In my diary I find the fol- lowing entry, "I must confess that I never enjoyed a holiday better and shall ever feel gratified to Mr E Milker for his kindness."

Cheshire Cheese.

At the monthly cheese fair, held at Whitchurch on Wednesday, there was a big pitch, over 60 tons, the quality of which, generally, was Rood, the pres- ent cool season being in faveur of makers. From the first a healthy tone prevailed,the best lots being picked up at prices which must have been satisfac- tory to the vendors, being fully equal to thou) of Chester the previous week, and from 5a to 6s per cwt better than at Whitchurch last month. Cheese of a medium quality were sold without any diffi- eulty,and,as a matter of fdct.every lot was disposed of within au hour from the time the bell rang. Factors were prePeet from Manchester, Liverpool, Birmingham, Wolverhampton, Chester, Crewe, Huddersfield, Stockport, Preston, and other parts of the country, and few, if any, returned without making purchases. Cheese if the finest qualities realised from 55s to 61s per cwt, and medium and lower grades from 45s to 545.

County.

Winwick Asylum for Chzonic Cases.

The Commissioners in Lunacy have issued their annual report to the Lord Chancel )r. her stress of canvas. A number of the peers seemed half inclined to swear at their resplendent finery, but there were some whose natural noblenees it could not bide, and some it even seemed to dignify. There were not many of these mighty folks I knew— Lord Salisbury, the noblest of them all, was absent, I was afterwards to learn ; I am confident I saw King Cole among the number, and our old friend Ally Sloper had undoubtedly contrived to get a coronet arid slip into the Abbey with the rest. The peeresses I ale() found a little disappointing. Their charms were very lavishly displayed, the coronets which sat so clumsily on many lordly nrow looked quite at home, amid their silken tresses ; but, though some were graceful auel a few were lovely, I would undertake to promptly match the best of them in ray own town. One poor young pest I noticed, a mere boy, was partly wale zed, and limped along with some great lady to his blazoned carriage with a pale, sad face which touched me to the quick. And one wee coroneted damsel in short frocks won my im- mediate homege, for she leoked the sweetest and simplest creature there. It had been My desire to crown my Coronation junket- inge by witnessing the naval review ; Lot, on the occa- sion of my previous visit, it had been postponed ; and, though I had a ticket for the pageant of the following Saturday, it would, I found, be quite impossible to use it. And so I had assiduously feasted on the railway guide in the vain hope that I might get to Portsmouth after witnessing the morning's ceremony, see the fleet lit up, and afterwards return to town in time for the Illurninatioria. On two occasions I had seen the assembled fleet before, but never in its livery of firs; and I erns greatly disappointed to discover that my project wipe not feasible. And so I slipped, iostead, as at tne Diamond Jubilee, to the familiar Crystal Palace, prompted mainly by the wish to see young Mr Spencer navigate his air-ship, and to watch the wonder- ful illuminations and the pyrotechnic pageantry with which this greatest monument of Edward's princely sire would fitly keep up the Ceronation Day. I saw the air- ship, and had a long chat with the galling zeronaut, was satisfied that he could safely steer it in 3 calm, and, as the lawns and Dathq and fountain burst into innumer- able gems of fire, I dined at a great window overlooking the enchanted scene, and drank King Edward's health with all tbe zest of one who, in his pardonable wish to see, had not found space Co eat or drink slime breakfast time ; returning, as the flaming splendours died away, through 000 l suburban gardens gay with fairy lamps and Chinese lanterns ' • and, at ten o'clock, I found myself proceeding up Constitution Hill, the searchlight on the tower of the great Catholic Cathedral, falling over Park and Palace in a silver beam, and ont inter a mystic thoroughfare which blazed from end to end with multitudinous illuminations. and was known, in ordinary times, as Piccadilly. St. James's-street, and . beautiful Pall Malawere each quite dazzling in their many coloured tributes to the King. St. James's Palace hap its fiery ornaments, and every street was packed with mighty crowds who slowly moved in two great lines In opposite directions, chinking in the radiant spectacle with eager eyes, and bursting here and there into delighted sung ; at times exuberant with the mere joy end beauty of it all ; but well-behaved and quiet in the main—a multitude of which the mightiest capital on earth might well be proud. I glanced into the busy Strand, and, passing on down Whitehall, reached the Thames Emeankment, where a thousand rainbow splen- dours were reflected in the heaving waters of the shadowy stream, and Cleopatra's Needle towered up to the night to speak of kings who had been dead six thouvand years. Afar, a fairy bridge reached out across the water, and a fiery castle loomed up to the stars, and wearily I walked towards them, through the cables streets ; but midnight was approaching now, and sud- denly they vanished like some shining mirage, and I found the 'Jay itself in darkness. But multitudee were pacing its greatlhoroughfares, and 'Arry and his 'Arriet everywhere, with concertinas and mouth organs, made the Sabbath morning hideous as, to mutual caresses and endearing words, they journeyed home. At ten past ten next day, I found myself within St ' Paul's Cathedral, once 'again, immediately below the great stone pulpit. The mighty edifice was packed from end to end. Erractly opposite there sat the Earl of Rose- bery, the one great nobleman who thought it worth his while to join in the momentous service. But Mr Bayard the American Ambassador,was there and Ras Makonnen and his suite, and, somewhere in the choir, were several members of the Reyal Family. Once more the Litany was sung in slow procession; oncerraore there passed in front of me a moving vision of high-planed ecclesias- tics, followed by the City Council and too scerlet-roberf ' Lord Mayor, and then the great thanksgiving service which had been so suddenly transformed, six weeks before, to one of supplication hushed the multitude to an unwonted silence ; the stately ritual proceeded the exulting Psalms were sung and then we listened with both hearts and ears to a i most suitable and moving sermon from the thin faced Bishop of London from the text commencing, " Thou ' beet turned my heaviness into joy." A striking utterance with its three notes of intense relief at the announce- menu that the King was crowned, of admiration for the courage he had manifested in the hour of his great eilal,' and of heartfelt gratitude at his recovery, and its out- spoken warning to the mighty Empire over which he ruled. Once before the words had been rung in our ears, " Lest we forget !" the preacher said. The lesson had been emphasized afresh, and he was fain to hope that we had taken it to heart. A glorious vision opened out to-day of a grateful King and a grateful people going forth together from a common trouble with a new belief in God, with a deepened sense of what both King and people owed to Hues, and with a clearer apprehension of the power of prayer, and a keener resolve to help one another to love God more. If that were the result, he seid, it would more than compensate for petty losses and for wasted decorations. The ever-beautiful Te Deum fol- lowed,and a number of the noteb'ee and lesser folk with- drew. The multitude remained, however, through the beautiful Communion Servicenhe members of the Royal Family partaking of the sacred bread and wise, and, after them, the congregation passing up to the high altar in a staady.and continuous stream. It was close on half-past one before the sacred rite was over. And then we all arose and sang " God save the King," and slowly drifted out into the common day again. Upon the threshold of the cheir, as I was sauntering round the noble building afterwards, I met the Rector, who was just as deeply moved as I by this most memorable service, which, Archdeacon Sinclair stated afterwards, was " quite spontaneous" and" in.anticiparioa of any- thing the King might do." Upon the day of supplication I had seen how Noncon- formity could rise to a great national event • and now I , felt that I should like to mingle with the Romaniedi I upon the day of national rejoicing. And so I went to Brompten Oratory, where, to clouds of incenee and a blaze of altar lights, a special le Deum surged and swelled to Heaven in honour of the Coronation. I never , heard more lovely music more divinely rendered. And, with the sound of it still echoing in my ears, and the sweet thought that Nonconformists, Anglicans, and Rornanists alike—however they might be divided other- wise—were absolutely one in their devotion to the Empire and the Throne, I hailed a han- som and drove bask to Easton. At my express de- sire, we passed the Palace once again. This sun was shining brightly now. The flags upon the roof were floating proudly forth. The King was resting happily wi:hin,his gratitude to God and to his people generously acknowledged by a nob's gift. Great crowds wale gathered round the miehty gates, and, quietly dis- mounting for a moment, I strolled up to read,

" Board of Green Cloth. Buckine,harn Palace, Sunday. " Rh Majesty bore the strain of the Coronation perfectly well, and experienced but little fatigue. " The King has had a good night, and his general condition is in every way satisfactory. No further bulletin will be issued. FREDERICK TROVES, FRANCIS IL LAKING." And then, with a glad 'aedrt, I hastened home.

Home from fhe Cape. On Tuesday evening over 200 men of the Ist South Lancashire and King's Liverpool Militia, who have servel at the Cape, reached the Central Station, Warrington. The men were in the best of spirits,and their lengthy stay in South Africa gave them a very bronzed appearance. Many of the soldiers brought back pets in the shano of parrots, &a. They were met by the band of the depot, and proceeded to the barracks, where they remained over night. A large crowd of relatives and friends witnessed their arrival.

Etttero to the Ebttor.

The The!wall Nuisance.

Sir,—ln this enlightened district we should be getting weary of protesting in vain against the conduct of our public affairs, and the stolid inaction of the Runcorn Rural District Council in the matter of the Thelwall Nuisanee, suggests that it is encouraged in its indiffer- ence by the apparent success of a similar policy on the part of the Warrington Corporation. In the case of the former, however, there is more to be considered than money wrongly spent, and the conflict between private interest and public advantage in the minds of men elected solely for the latter cause. Bad as this is, it must give place to the greater evil, whereby a body of men not only risks, but actually sacrifices human life, and when protest is made, mumbles inanely that the medical officer is satisfied there is nothing wrong. Are we to wait until the whole district affected is depopulat- ed:by diphtheria before these men will bestir themselves? Are they quim heartless that they can see a man's chil- dren die one by one, and remain unmoved ? If death called at their own homes, they would take action quickly enough, and one wonders at the elfishness and inhuman indifference that can allow a danger such as unquestionably exists at Thelwall to go unrernedied— nay, wer3e—can affirm that no evil exists. Is our old enemy "private interest in public men" responsible for this ? Mr R A Naylor is not without influence, but the Council ignores his frequent appeals, and meets with foolish laughter thesatternpt of Admiral Jones-Parry to convince them of the seriousness of their position. Meantime there is still another serious case of diph- theria at Thelwall, and in my opinion, the men who as members of the Council display such callous indifference under such circumstances, are liter- ally responsible for the deaths which occur. As however the law cannot touch them, we may pre sume they will rest satisfied and self-righteous, and put down the disease-ridden state of the district to the "Act of God "or possibly of the devil, and the latter may be nearer the mark, but some of us prefer to call it the Runcorn Rural District Council.

It is a thousand pities that a man like Mr R A Naylor who by his personal efforts and his generosity has so often berefited not only his immediate neighbours but also the town of WarrinZon, should he driven from amongst us. He is highly esteemed bore, and can feel assured of the sympathy of every " live " man and woman in the district and if we can only waken some of the sleepy ratepayers, we shall yet smarten up both the Runcorn Rural District Council and the Warrington Corporation. and so we hope some day to welcome Mr. Nayl )r hack to an un- polluted Thelwall and a progressive Warrington. The reproach of indifference has long rested on the town,but Mr. Naylor has done much for 119, and we should be ready in turn to back him up in his efforts to bring about sane and sanitary methods in the matter of the Thelwall nuisance. This WP sbould recognize as a personal claim of his, hut with death and sickness at one very doors we mutt recognize a vastly greater claim and in the name of common humanity use what influence we have to end the nuisance. Yours truly, Warrington, August 27th, 1902.

===== 0002601_19020829_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
VOX EX URBE.

Poverty in our Great Towns.—

Proposed Further Investigations.

Sir,—Now that the echoes of war have passed away, it may be hoped that the attention of serious men and women may again be directed to that most grave and distressing of all the problems that face us—the persis- tence of acute poverty in our large towns. That a quarter of the industrial population of England, at the moment of her greatest power and highest wealth, should lack the wherewithal of bare physical efficiency would be incredible but for facts brought to light in recent investigations. That " darkest London" remains, a standing rebuke to our National pretensions, every social worker in the Metropolis knows. But who would have imagined before Mr. B. S. Rowntree carried out his inquiry into tbe condition of the people in rho city of York that an apparently prosperous provincial town would show as black a record. It is very satisfactory, as far as it goes, to notice that Mr Rowntrae's very remarkable account of this inquiry and its results— " Poverty : A study of Town Life "—has made a deep impression on the public mind, all preoccupations not- withstanding. But—it may be asked—how far can we rely upon these resblts as of general application ? Mr Rowntree deals with this question; and MrCharles Booth an unquestionable authority praises the work as one of general value. But though this may be taken as established, there must be large variations in local con- ditions ; it is clearly desirable that in every large town of the provinces th:re should be made an investigation of a like character. Perhaps the most useful feature of Mr Rowntree's book is that it. points the way—it is a re- cord of methods, as well as of results,a guide to all those who are interested in the details—in housing, or food, or temperance, or sanitation, or education—as well as in the general aspect of the social problem. Sweral provincial conferences ha‘e already been held in:partimlar, a meeting,including many prominent local men, took place last spring in the Manchester Town Hall under the presidency of the Lord Mayor. What the Citizens' Committee is now doing in Manchester could be done more easily and perhaps more successfully In smaller towns. May I suggest, therefore, that the churches and social bodies of Warrington should con eider whether it would be useful to hold a conference with a view to a scientific investigation of the condition of the town in regard to roverty, insanitation, sickness, etc. during the coming autumn and winter? lam sure Mr Rowntree,Mr Marr (of the Manchester Citizens' Com- mittee), and other experienced investigators will will- ingly give helpful advice. Faithfully yours, Lucien-road, Tooting, Loudon, S. W.

G. Ef.'PERRIS.

1.0.G.T., South-West Lancashire District Lodge, The August quarterly session was held on Satur day last, in the Congregational school, Newton street. Leigh. Bro the Rey J A Lee. DC T (Liverpool) presided over a good number of representative and ' visitors from Warrington, Liverpool, Earlestown, Southport and other parts of South-West Lanca- shire. After the routine business of the meeting had been transacted, the DC T presented his report. He said he was deeply disappointed that again he had to report a decrease and he feared that unless strenuous efforts were made, they would not finish the year in as good a position as 1902 found them. He urged the members to be " up and doing," the enemy they had to contend with was crafty and powerful. In these days of Public House Trusts and other combinations, Good Templary must not be inactive. He saw in the question of Public House Trusts a real danger; while a well-conducted public house is better than a badly-conducted one, yet he feared this will not be secured by interest- ing more people in their five per cent shares under the colour of philanthropy. The D Sec (Bro Morris Jones, Liverpool) in his report, stated that there were now 2323 members in the district in 51 lodges. Two lodges had been re-opened during the quarter including the Temperance Mission lodge which is now doing good work in WArrington. Bro 0 B Walker, D Treasurer (Liverpool) reported a good balance in hand. Bro E Newman, DSJ T (Earlestown), reported on the work among the , juveniles. He was pleased to say that the temples fthroughout the district were progressing favour- ably and he had received an invitation to re-open the Young Mission Temple, Warrington, which has done good work in the past and he trusted that the Temple would prosper and help to stem the tide of drunkenness in the town. The reports were adopted, together with the reports of the Executive and Credential Committees. It was thsn decided to hold the November session in Earlestown under the auspices of the Hope of Earlestown lodge. The district lodge degree was conferred upon eleven members. Addresses were then delivered by Bro Cox, PGAS (Manchester).and Bro Fulton, D Chap (South East banes), and Bro Thomas presented a book of references to the Executive Committee The usual votes of thanks brought the meeting to a c 10,13 After tea, a rousing open-air meeting was held when there was a good audience and it is be- lieved that good work was accomplished.

In Swansea,on;Wednesday a young woman carrying a six-months-old child entered into a casual co oversation with a number of women. One of the women admired the child, and remarked that she wished it was her own. "You can have it if you like," was the reply. The offer was at once accented, and the foster-mother left with her adopted child. Later on she was seen buying clothes for her new charge.

PICTURESQUE PAGEANT. -_ The Fancy Dress Parade and Carnival at Warrington.

Warrington was the scene on Saturday evening of probably tne most picturesque pageant which has ever traversed its streets—certainly for many years back. Variety, originality, and artistic taste, combined to produce a spectacle, pleasant remem- brances of which will long be retained by all who witnessed it. The occasion, it need hardly be stated, was the grand fancy dress parade and carnival promoted by the Warrington Cycling Club, to aid two of our most valuable institutions— the Infirmary and District Nursing Association. The members of the club, assisted by members of other organizations, had thrown themselves heart and soul into the project. For weeks past, various sub- committees had been at work, determined to leave no stone unturned to ensure complete success. "To help the suffering " was the one desire which ani- mated the promoters, and could any desire be more commendable ? The noble work carried on by the Infirmary and District Nursing Association is too well-known to call forth any comment. here. The working-classes are the people who derive the greatest benefit from the institutions, and the parade afforded them a means of sub;cribing some- thing, however little, towards the funds necessary for the maintenance of the work. This is not the first occasion on which the Cycling Club have arranged a parade with the object of assisting local charities. Two years ago a similar parade took place, and was attended with magnificent success, £365 being raised. It was hoped that this year even greater sucz.ess would be achieved, but it must be borne in mind that in 1900 a watch competition was the means of realizing over £lOO, and it was decided not to have a competition in connection with this year's parade. It is gratifying to be able to state that the parade was a splendid success, and everything points to the most sanguine hopes of the promoters being realized. The promoters had succeeded in obtaining the services of a large and enthusiastic band of collectors who did their work in a per- fectly satisfactory manner, the realization of the handsome sum of .£240 being the result of their evertions. Many ladies also undertook to solicit subscriptions, apart from the street collections, but the amount derived from this source is not yet known.

Saturday opened very unpropitiously for an out door spectacle, rain falling very heavily during the morning, and it seemed at one time that the parade was doomed to failure. Happily, the conditions took a complete change for the better in the after- noon, and at the time appointed for the assembly of the processionists in the park the sun was shining brilliantly, and there was every promise of a fine evening. rho roads, however, were damp and soft, and rendered the lot of the paraders rather unpleas- ant, several of the ladies' dresses being besmirched with mud. _ . .

By five o'clock the streets were lined with specta- tors who eagerly awaited the start of the parade. The approach of the procession was heralded by a gaily-decorated motor-car containing a number of officials in fancy costumes representing pantomime characters. An announcement on the car enjoined spectators to "get their money ready." The work of marshalling having been completed the first section of the parade moved off, the chief marshal (Mr H Ashton) occupying the foremost position. Police and Volunteers comprised this section. The Volunteer Band played lively music, and the mounted Infantry in their scarlet tunics and hats with cock-tail feathers, presented a smart appearance on their prancing steeds. The Volunteer Cycle Corps followed immediately behind. An interesting feature was the representation of the "Old Bluebacks " and other types of byegone vol- unteers in Warrington. Section two was headed by the Penketh Tannery band and included the members of the Royal Cycle Club in costume characters and with decorated machines. This club succeeded in gaining the sec- retary's prize for the largest muster in the parade The humorous element was supplied by the impersonation of " Weary Willie and Tired Tim" who were mounted on an antiquated machine, and whose antics created a deal of mirth. Other cuaracters worthy of note were a monk with his "St. Bernard " dog, " China-up-to-date," ar.d an armed Kaffir. Miss May Warren, of Stockton Heath. displayed much originality in her represen- tation of ping-pong. on her head was a miniature ping-pong table and racquet, whilst her dress was , ornamented with vane-coloured ping-pong balls, , Superintendent Turner and the members of the Fire Brigade figured in this section, the engines, which were arawn by powerful draught horses, being prettily decorated. A number of the , firemen were very energetic in collecting coppers, one utilizing a fire shovel for this purpose. The band of Messrs Joseph Crosfield and Sons. Ltd, headed section three and the firm had an attractive tableau representing " Washing day." As illustrating the work of the St John Ambulance Corps, one of the lurries contained two youths who hadreceived"first aid"after:meeting with an accident as the result of reckless riding on a machine which seemed to have got the worst of a collision. The part which the Corps has played in the recent war was also illustrated, a boy clad in khaki and swathed in bandages being wheeled on a stretcher. A character which attracted general attention was a very tasteful and original represen- tation of a "Shower of Gold" by Miss Bond, of Southport. This lady's costume glittered with hun- dreds of imitation sovereigns and one was reminded of the old saying "Allis not gold that glitters." Her machine was adorned with golden chrysanthe- mums and streaming from an open parasol was a "shower of gold." An exceedingly humorous char- acter was" A general nuisance "portrayed by Mr J J Pritchard, who was clad in an amazing garb of sacking, &c. Another cyclist, covered with band- ages, and wheeling a badly damaged machine, dis- played a card with the words" Help the infirmary, it has helped me." The amusing feature of this section was the " Brannigan's band" from St Hilda's Mission, Latchford, who produced ear- splitting sounds from instruments " which included horns, tin cans, &c. An original character was depicted by Mr J Marshall, Southport, who , appeared as a "cycling monkey" and displayed ability ass trick-rider. The Pioneer (late St Paul's) band supplied the music for section four. The Richmond Gas Stove and Meter Company, Ltd., had an exhibit advertis- ing one of their specialities known as " Richmond's Kitchener gas stove." Lord Kitchener was seated at a table evidently occupied in the perusal I of documents, and the "make-up " was a very I creditable one. Roars of laughter were created by some Penketh enthusiasts who represented the Darktown Fire Brigade. The fire engine consisted of a barrel mounted on wheels, and the announce- ment "Pierpoint Morgan offered £lOO,OOO for this engine. Refused I" was prominently displayed. Another card bore the information that the next fire would take place at the Parr Hall at 730. The Cycling Club from Orford Barracks looked very spic and span in their uniforms. Fallow- ing them was a host of characters, humorous and otherwise. One which excited considerable amusement was the " sentimental dude "who stood seven feet high and showed at least one foot of collar. A novel "get-up " repre- sented a figure upside down. Another parader was attired in a ragged costume whilst on his head was a large crown. This individual, it appeared, had been " coronated by mistake" A distinguishing feature of the fifth section, was the performance of the Crewe Royal Morris Dancers. As they paraded the streets they delighted the on- lookers with the dance which has been so long associated with May day and village festivals. They wore becoming costumes of red and white and danced along to the accompaniment of jingling bells. The Borough band played appropriate dance music. The Manchester Ship Canal Company bad an effective tableau. A large boat was conveyed through the streets on a lorry, and one of the occu- pants was costumed to represent Father Neptune. Around him were a number of " sailors " who in- vited the spectators to throw their money into the boat. .The appropriate announcement " Nentune from the Ship Canal seeks aid for the sick " was displayed. A very pretty costume was that of Miss Elsie Birch all. who represented a " willow pattern serenader." Other interesting characters were the " New Woman," and "Dan Leno," whilst consider- able originality was displayed by a processionist who wore a suit which was completely covered with cigarette cards. The hand of Messrs Peter Walker and Son, Ltd., led off section six, and behind it marched a contin- gent of hove from People's College clad inkhaki and carrying rifles. Much laughter was created by the "Manx twins " who were dressed as schoolboys and carried on their backs a slate bearing the sentence "From the pig we get pig iron." The " Blew hungry " band from Tyldesley was a great attrac-

===== 0002601_19020829_art0132.txt | included=true | included =====
tion. One of its members had a card on his back with the lines " Bid me discourse and I will en- chant thine ear." When the band did discourse, however, the effect was far from enchanting. Dis- tracting would be a more accurate description. The seventh section was headed by the band of Messrs Fletcher, Russell and Company, Limited, and that firm contributed several attractive features to the parade. The most noteworthy was a miniature tram car complete with trolley pole, and the onlookers were requested to " throw their fares." There was also a lurry containing gas stoves, &c., and a set dinner table surrounded by several' chefs. A very original charaeer was "Jack Straw" (Mr W Statham) who was swathed in straw wrappings, and other cyclists were attired as representatives of various nations. Mr R Alshall, Everton, who secured the first prize for the most original charac- ter on foot,represented a "Mussulman," his costume being entirely covered with mussel-shells. The Runcorn Cycling Club, with "special Scotch band," brought up the rear. The League of the Cross Band was at the bead of section eight, and the Junior dnim and fife band of the League followed. Then came more cyclists in costume, and many and varied were the " make-ups." The Winwick Asylum Fire Brigade with their cycles made a smart turnout. There was another lurry sent by the Manchester Ship Canal Co.. on which were seated divers in full costume with attendants. The ninth section of the parade was headed hr the Blue Coat School Band and the members of the Warrington Cycling Club walked in this section. The veteran "Pa " Davies appeared as" Uncle Sam." Two paraders had costumes covered with oyster shells, one being labelled " a real native " and the other "a walking oyster saloon." Other characters included a naval officer and pierrots. Padgate Industrial School Band discoursed music. Legh-street Subscription Band headed the last section. A character which " took " immensely with the crowd was a skit on the weather we have recently been experiencing. Messrs Picton, of Grappe,nhall, clad in winter costumes trimmed I with fur, walked alongside a tandem covered with cotton wool (snow). An open umbrella, also covered with cotton wool, bore the words " Summer, 1902." Miss A Ellison, Warring- ton, was tastefully attired to represent "Coronation decorations"her dress being adorned with miniature flags, fairy lamps, &c., whilst her machine was decorated with materials of patriotic colours. Mr F White, ludi3rously attired and mounted on stilts had a card on his back with the inscription" rhis season's catch", Mr E Battersby, of Liverpool, who was clad as athlete had an ingeniously constructed model on'a machine called pot hunting.lon one side there was a miniature athletic ground, and on the other a pinnacle of fame leading to which were ladders. There was also a small house with a card giving the information " Full of prizes." Other representations were Captain Kettle, Robin Hood, convicts and warder, Mephis topheles, and the Duke of Buckingham." Last came Brannigan's Band, from Earlestown, and it was generally agreed that of all the comic bands they took the palm. The cornet players thought nothing of each playing a different tune, and one performer was blowing through the wrong end of his instru- ment. The drummer was especially energetic, but did not always hit the drum, the bandsmen in the immediate vicinity having at times to beat a retreat Fortunately the weather continued fine during the whole of the evening, and the parade passed off without the slightest hitch* The route was a long one and was crowded with spectators almost from end to end. There was a consensus of opinion that apart from the object, the parade itself was well worth paying to see, and the collectors reaped a good harvest. A number of firms had lent lurries for the reception of contributions. THE CONCERT AND PRIZE DISTRIBUTION,. _

PROCEEDS.

The proceeds of the street collection amounted to the very handsome sum of £240, which was a i increase of £7O over the amount raised by this means two years ago. Of this £192 Is 4d was subscribed in coppers.

THE NORT HERN COUNTIES AIA, AND N.U. FOOTBALLERS.

An Objection Laid Against Fish.

The athletic season is not to pass away without another attempt by the Executive of the Northern Counties Athletic Association to deal with Northern Union footballers vitdo have competed and carried away prizes under their auspices. At the end of last season the credentials of Bone, the Salford threequarter, were questioned by a runner named Smith, of Harpurhey. On that occasion the Association dismissed the charge. Is is now understood that objection has been laid to Fish, of Warrington, who has been very successful on the track of late. The athletic authorities are ' finding themselves face to face with a serious I dilemma over this question of Northern Union ; players competing at their sports. Their only remedy seems to be to app;y to the Northern Union secretary for information as to whether certain suspected men are I registered as football professionals. The Union in I question is in no way bound to supply such information I and then the onus of proof that this is so rests upon the Association, which has always been the case The athletic authorities have had to confess themselves baffled up to now as to how to treat with the cases, and it is by no means a remote probability that during the ensuing winter some legislation will be effected putting a complete ban upon the athletic status of all Northern Union players. Meanwhile the latter have for years been carrying off prizs with impunity, and Vernon Hampson, of Swinton, still remains the scapegoat of the whole system, he having been under suspension for a couple of seasons.

RUNCORN SCHOOL BOARD.

The monthly meeting of the above authority was held on Wednesday evening. Mr Pritchard was in the chair and there were also present Rev Father Chambers Messrs F J Norman, W Handley, R LI Posnett, J Littler and A Lightburn (acting clerk).

- RESIGNATIONS. The School Management Coannithae reported accounts passed for poymcnt amounting to £597 15 Id. Met re- signations of Miss Nellie McDona!d and WSP Annie Dyer were accepted, The committee recommended the pur- chase of four new desks for the pupil teachers centre. The recommendations were approved.

THE ANNUAL GRANTS AND REPORTS.

Mr Handley read the following reports from His Majesty's Inspector re the schools under the Board. Runcorn, Granville-street :—" This school is greatly im- proved in discipline and has made a corresponding ad- vance in instruction. It is now doing well." Mill Brow :—" This school is in good order and continues to do very areditably." The evening school : " This is a useful evening school doing good work." Victoria Boys :—"Both discipline and instruction are thoroughly satisfactory." Girls :—" The girls are in very good order. The work is generally good. Handwriting is often poor in the first and second class. Composition is much better. A. sample course of lessons should be arranged on the knowledge of common things. The school accommodation is at present insufficient for the average attendance. This should be at once remedied or the fee 'scant next year will be in danger." Con- tinuing Mr Handley said the reports were very satisfac- tory. A copy of the Victoria girls report was sent to the head teacher who would no doubt take a note of the remarks made. Last year the accommodation. at Victoria Schools was for 444 and the average attendance was 451. In the girls department the accommodation was for 381 with an average attendance of 395. The figures were not up to the recognised standard. The grants for the different schools were as follows :—Mill Brow, full grant. 117 at 17s, £99 93 : fee grant, £5B lOs ; total, £157 193. Granville.street, 144 at 17s, £122 Bs ; pupil teachers, £2 ; fee grant, £72 ; total, £196 184. Victoti..-road, boys 450 at 225, £496 2e; Pupil teachers, £2 ; fee grant, £423 103 ; total, £1,358 2s. Total for all schools, £1,712 93. The number of hours recognized as teaching hours was 9,568 at 3s per 90 hours which gave £7l 16 The special subjects, chemistry, home nursing were 751 hours at 43 per 20 hours, which gave £7 63. Mr Norman suggested that a storey be put on the end of the Victoria-road schools. Mr Handley pointed out that only a roof was required as the walls were already there. It would be a great convenience. The report was approved.

Good Templary—Re-opening of a Juvenile Temple. _ .

On Wednesday evening, the "Young Mission of Warrington "Juvenile Temple was re-opened by Sister Mrs Woods, CSJT (Marlestown),after sleep- ing for about 18 months. The meeting was held in the Ebenezer Mission Room, Buttermarket-street, Twenty-six juvenile members were initiated and eleven adult members joined the temple. The officers were installed and instrueted in their various duties, after which Bro Newman, DSJ T, and Sister Mrs Woods.o SJ T, (Ear- lestown), addressed the children, the former promising a medal to the member introducing the most candidates during the quaVer. Sister Mrs Sheath was recommended as S JT. and Bro W Griffiths as A S J T.—The members of the adult lodge held their weekly session in the same room. Bro Merlin, C T presided over a good attendance. After the usual business, Bro Roberts, P C T, made , a very sympathetic reference to the death of Bro W Sutherland, G Sec (London) and moved that a vote of condolence be sent to Mrs Sutherland and family in their sad bereavement. Bro O'Brien, in a very touching speech,seconded the resolution which was ably supported by Bro Newman, D S J T. and carried by all the members standing. Sister Woods of Earlestown, gave a brief address, congratulating the members on the re-opening of the Juvenile , temple and said she was pleased that so many adult members had taken the four-fold pledge. Bro Newman related some interesting anecdotes con- nected with Good Templarv. Bros J Sheath and Roberts gave reports of the district lodge , meetings of South West Lancashire and E and M Cheshire respectively. Bro O'Brien re- lated some interesting reminiscences re his visit to South East Yorkshire. The following programme was then rendered :—Glee " Each gentle dove." Templar Glee Party ; duet," Down in the valley," Sister S L Sheath and Bro C Clegg ; cornet solo, "Let me like a soldier fall," Bro W Griffiths ; reci- tation, " Yin'. the hero of the slums," Sister G Hughes ; song "Old folks at home " (with vocal banjo accompaniment), Sister K Bowyer ; glee, "Ringing cheerily," Glee party ; recitation, "Little Blossom " Sister M E Hughes ; song, "The midslaipmite," Bro O'Brien; round, "Home to dinner," glee party. WATER SUPPLY AT THE'LWALL-.

The Manchester Ship Canal Company wrote as follows on August 20th : We understand that your Council are laying a main for supplying water to the houses in the village of The!wall. The Canal Company own two houses close to The!wall Ferry,which are at the present time de- pendent for their water supply on a well situated at Mr Whitlow's farm on the opposite side of the lane. The waterldrawn from this well is very unsatisfactory and the tenant of the farm is now being supplied:by town's water which will no doubt lead to his well being condemned. We shall be glad to know whether your Council will be prepared to continue the main as far as the two houses, in which case the Canal Company will be prepared to:pay the cost of putting in the connection. We believe they are the only two houses which are not supplied with town's water.

Mr Farrington reported that the length of the exten- sion of the water main was about 47 yard's. If that was done the Canal Company would have to lay a surface pipe 80 yards in length. The extension of the main would cost £7.

It was decided to carry out the extension on the motion of Admiral Parry, seconded by Mr Roylance.

TREATMENT OF CONSUMPTION.

The County Council forwarded copies of the report of the Conference with reference to consumptive sanatoria under the Isolation Hospitals Acts held at Crewe, A resolution similar to the one approved by the Board taf Guardians was passed, on the motion of Mr Wood- ward, seconded by Mr Linaker. - ACCOMMODATION POR CORPSES AT ASTON. . _

Mr Robert Dobson, Coroner for Halton, wrote on July nth stating that on the previous day he held an inquest at Sutton Weaver on the body of a man unknown which had been found in the river Weaver. The body which was much decomposed was placed in a shed or building belonging to the licensee of the Aston Arms Hotel where bodies generally are placed which are found in the river or canal in that neighbourhood. The shed was near a cottage occupied by John Moss who complained very much of offensive smell from the dead body. The Inspector of Nuisances appeared to have done all he could to mitigate the nuisance. He (the coroner) was writing at the suggestion of the jury to say that it would be well, if poLsible, to provide a more suitable place where todies awaiting inquest could be placed. The Clerk said he was still corresponding with the Weaver Trustees on the matter.

The question was adjourned. -- A PUBLIC DANGER..-THE DINGLE BRIDGE.

Mr Dobson also wrote on August 13th iwith-7eference to the inquest held on John Hindley of Appleton who was killed by falling from the Dingle Bridge into the brook. The depth from the bridge to the brook was about fifteen feet and the height of the wall of the bridge was only twenty two inches above the roadway. The wall was so low that it was dangerous to drive a restive horse over the bridge at any time and there was a pro- bability of foot passengers in the dark missing their way and falling over. A man did fall from tbe bridge into a bush happily without fatal injuries about a year ago. The jury recommendedjthat the walls at the sides of the bridge should be considerably raised as a safeguard to the public using the road and bridge. Mr Diggle said the walls originally wire 2 feet 9 inches high but the road having been raised they were now only 1 foot 10 inches. The bridge had been dangerous for some time especially to children. He suggested that an unclimbable iron railing be erected at a cost of £25. The bridge appeared to have been built by the Appleton Hall estate. Ir was decided on the motion of Mr Linaker, seconded by Admiral Parry, that the Surveyor produce • plan of the proposed alterations at the next meeting. WARRINGTON LIGHT RAILWAY.

The Light Railway Commissioners forwarded copy of the following letter which had been addressed by the Clerk of the Stockton Heath Parish Council in respect of Clause 63 of the Warrington Corporation Light Railway Order, as provisionally drafted by the Commissioners : --" I am directed by the Light Railway Commissioners to inform you that with reference to the proposed Clause 63 of the Order to authorise the above-named Light Railway of which they have issued their provisional draft, they are advised that the proposal to give effect by way of enactment under the Order to the opinion expressed by the Commissioners in favour of a maximum Penny fare between Rylands-street, Warrington and Victoria-square, Stockton Heath, is open to objection On behalf of the Corporation of Warrington it has been recently pointed out to the Commissions and insisted upon that the right of the Corporation to take charges on their existing tramways is settled by Act of Parlia- ment 63 and 64 Victoria, and that the special fare pro- posed in so far as it related to part of the journey over the trarnways would be in contravention of such statutory rights. The Commissioners do not think it proper under the circumstances to retain the proviso to Clause 63 referred to. They will, however, provide that for the purpose of calculating distances the tramway and the light railway shall be deemed in the case of a journey partly on one and partly on the other to be the same line. In connection with this matter lam further directed to remind you that while the Corporation have objected to an obligatory maximum fare, they, by their representative at the local inquiry expressed the hope that they would in practice be able to limit themselves to the charge, and the commissioners therefore anticipate that if it should be found by the corporation to be practicable to carry passengers from Stockton Heath to Rylands-street for a penny fare, they will dal it. The Light Railway Commissioners are pro- ceeding to settle the order for submission to the Board of Trade but as this point was one to which the Parish of Stockton Heath had attached importance and as to which the commissioners bad expressed themselves in accord with the Parish I have been directed to give you this early intimation that the provisional draft of the order will have to be amended in that particular. (Signed) Alan D. Erskine.

TUE TUELWALL NUISANCE. The Local Government Board wrote under date August 15th. with reference to the piping of the ditch in the parish of Grappenhall inquiriog what had been done in the matter and whether the action taken had been effectual in putting an end to the serious nuisance com- plained of. _ _ . _

In reply to Mr Linaltrr, Mr Swinton the surveyor,said the work was carried out so far as the sewer was con- cerned. He was awaiting further instructions from someone to continue the sewer further.

The Clerk said the owners of the Belmont estate and the occup!ers of the Tannery asked that a trap should be placed at the top end of the sewer, and he took it that the medical officer of Health would recommend that that be done.

Mr Linaker understood that the whole thing had been mottled with the owners of the Belmont estate and the tannery.

The Surveyor said the first scheme Watl to place a syphon so as to check thr swell returning. Messrs Lone said they would do without it, and now they were ask- ing for the thing to be done.

Mr Linaker : The whole thing is a disgrace to the Connell.

Admiral Parry : Hear, bear. Mr Wallington : When it is completed there will be a certain amount of pressure of gas. Dr Adams : There is a nine inch ventilator to relieve the gas:

Mi. Wallington asked if the gas emanating therefrom was injurious. Dr Adams as3ured him that no harm would come from it.

Mr Barton inquired bow long the work had been stopped

Tfie Surveyor : Two month. (Cries of " Shame.") Mr Roylance asked if anything had been done with a view to carrying out the work during the last two months ?

The Surveyor replied he had made out plans with the time but bad received no reply from the trustees of the estate.

Mr Roylance : Has Dr Adams done anything. Dr Adams • I had nothing to do with it. Mr Roylance : Has the clerk done anything P The Clerk : I have written twenty to thirty letters hastening on the work. The only thing that required doing was the inserting of a trap at the top end of the sewer to prevent the smell going into Messrs Longs. Dr Adams suggested that an intercepting tank should be erected to deal with the sewage. After some further discussion it was ultimately resolved that the surveyor be instructed to carry out the work on the motion of Messrs Roylance and Wood- ward. A FATAL CASE OF DIPHTHERIA AT THELWALL. Dr vactier. county medical Officer of Health wrote en- closing letters ftom Mr RA Naylor of Cuerden Hall. Thelwall, with reference to the death from diphtheria of his coachman's child. He should be pleased to consider any particulars which might be ascertained by investi- gation and any report made upon the case by the Medical Officer of Health. _ _

In his first letter Mr Naylor writing from London on August 16th. drew the doctor's a,tention to the illness of the child. He said that as sure as night follows day it I was with these poisonous fumes. They spoke the truth but no one would believe them and no one would help I them. Admiral Jones-Parry tried his best with the Council on Tuesday but from what he gathered from his letter he could not get a hearing. What poor people would do when the winter came God only knew. Even the flowers sent from there were saturated with the poison, and his sister and one of the servants had that week been made seriously ill with what were sent. They had told their gardener to send no more. They are filled with the tannery odours. On the following day Mr Naylor wrote stating that the child was dead. The letter concluded ; "I wanted

them to leave che house and come hare, but as a matter of duty they stayed. Alas ! how horrible their ta aerinee' for faithful service."

Apropos of the same subject Mr It A Naylor wrote as followe under date August 25th from London :—Re- ferring, to your assistant's letter of July 28th, lam still without the information asked for. I also desire to know why a copy of this report was omitted from your annual record of last year. There is something very irregular about your proceedings and I must ask you to ascertain at to-morrow's meeting what explanation your Council has to off Er. I noticed at one of your recent meetings you and your co-officials did your best to insult me. This from public :servants is highly im- proper and as you are paid from the public purse, although we oannot compliment you upon efficiency, still we do expect ordinary civility. On the same question Mr F !Leah, wrote from South. port, again complaining of " the vile stench" which they had all along maintained emanated from the tannery at Thelwall. They had gone to Southport to get a breath of fresh air, as, on the previous day(A.ugust 23rd) there was a keen acrid stench, biting one's throat, all over Cuerden Hall premises which came direct from the tannery. As parents they begged them to retnedY the terrible evil if in their power to do so. A desultory discussion ensued after the reading of the letters but uothing was resolved on.

TUE HEALTH OF THE DISTRICT. With regard to the fatal case at Thelwall the cause of the complaint was not known but an investigation would loa made in due course.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS. Cricket. Warrington always find powerful opponents in the Leyland eleven, which includes a good number of players who have done duty for various counties, and they did well in making a draw of the match on Satur- day. The Leyland batsmen were in capital form, and the closure was applied when the aggregate had reached 143 for the loss of seven wickets. Warrington had an hour and twenty minutes in which to save the match, and when play ceased they had put together 56 for the loss of only three wickets. Wilmot, who has kept wicket for Derbyshire, was the most successful Leyland batsman his innings realizing 43. The other chief contributors were J Stanning (22), Kershaw (20), J. E. Stanning (17) and Tinsley (13). J. W. Bibby and B. Sileock deserve the credit for the savin! of the match. When they commenced their innings Warrington had three wickets down for 13 runs, so that the prospects did not look very bright Several bowlers were tried, but the batsmen kept their wickets intact, and at the close Bibby had compiled 27 and Silcock 13. Warrington Wesleyans easily defeated Latchford Bap- tists at Liverpool-road. The visitors were dismissed for 25, and, having knocked up 36 for the loss of one wicket, the Wesleyans retired. Doody bowled splen- didly for the winners, capturing seven wickets for three runs. Light (18 not out) andF Taylor (14 not out) batted well. -- Angling. A match was arranged by the committee of Kin,,o• Edward VII Coronation Club of Jolly Anglers and fished at Thelwall, for extra prizes, for the unsuccessful com- petitors at last week's match. Nineteen competed, and the results were as follows :-1, le Parry ; 2, J Payne ; 3, William Shaw; 4, F Atkinson. The above club are sending two teams to compete to-day (Saturday) for the Warrington Angling Association's shield.

The Gerard Family. Since the death of the late Lord Gerard the Gerard family have decided to let Eastwell Park, Ashford, Kent, for one or mere years. It will be remembered that it was at Eastwell Park that Lord Gerard died, being removed to Garewood Hall, Garswood, Newton-le-Willows, Lancashire, after- wards for burial. Garswood Hall, and not East- well Park, is the ancestral seat of the Gerarda of Brynn, Eastwell only being purchased by the late Lord Gerard. The son, though succeeding to the title at once, will not cacti"' his majority until 1904, so that Eastwell is evidently to be let until the pre- I sent Lord Gerard it of age. His Majesty the King ' has been the guest of the late Lord Gerard at both , Eastwell Park and Garswood Hall. Lady Gerard, ' with her daughter, the Hon. Ethel Gerard, are at present staying at Westgate - The Departure of Wesleyan Ministers' The Rev Theodore Bishop and the Rev C H Hocken, prior to their departure for Llandudno and Derby respectively, received several presents in addition to those mentioned on our last issue- Mr. Hocken was the recipient of a gold-mounted silk umbrella, with a suitable inscription from the mem- bers of his society class at a tea and meeting arranged by Mrs and Miss Bailey. Mrs Hocken was presented with a beautiful hana-bag by the mem- bers of her class, the presentation being made by Miss Healey and Miss Skelland. Mr Hocken was also the recipient of a handsome electro-plated afternoon tea service in the Queen Anne style from the members of the Mothers:Meeting in connection with Bewsey-road. This presentation was made by Mrs William Parkinson and Miss Lily Shaw- Green Besides these gifts presented in public, a number of valuable presents were given to Mr and Mrs Bishop aud Mr and Mrs Hocken by friends privately.
